Table 4.1
:
Total hypotheses (Hyps.) and rejections (Rejs.) per physiological quantity using our causal bounds, as well as those of
Manski 1990

We obtained rejections for hypotheses corresponding to 10 different physiological quantities shown in Table
4.1
.
(Table
8.3
in the Appendix shows all hypotheses we tested, including those not rejected.)
We may therefore infer that, at a high level, Pulse does not simulate these quantities accurately for the population of sepsis patients we consider.
This appears of interest in a variety of downstream settings: for example, a developer could use this information when considering how to improve the accuracy of Pulse, while a practitioner using Pulse may wish to rely less on these outputs as a result.

To assess the relative performance of our bounds from Theorem
4.4.1
compared with the unconditional bounds of
Manski 1990
, we also reran this analysis with each
t
,
f
, and
a_{1:t}
chosen as before, but with each
B_{0:t}
now set to the whole space
\mathcal{X}_{0:t}
.
This in turn led to fewer hypotheses, namely 690 in total, which were evenly divided between hypotheses of the form
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{Y}(a_{1:t})]\geq\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}]
and those of the form
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{Y}(a_{1:t})]\leq\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{up}}]
.
We kept all other aspects of our procedure the same as just described, including our method for obtaining confidence intervals and controlling for multiple testing.
The number of rejections that we obtained in this case is also shown in Table
4.1
.
As anticipated by our discussion in Section
4.4.2
, the conservativeness of Manski’s bounds led to considerably fewer rejections than our more general result given in Theorem
4.4.1
, even when considered as a proportion of the total hypotheses we tested.

4.6.3
p-value plots

To obtain more granular information about the failure modes of the twin just identified, we examined the
p
-values obtained for each hypothesis
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{up}}
tested using our causal bounds, which we denote here by
p_{\textup{lo}}
and
p_{\textup{up}}
.
Figure
4.1
shows the distributions of
-\log_{10}{p_{\textup{lo}}}
and
-\log_{10}{p_{\textup{up}}}
that we obtained for all physiological quantities for which some hypothesis was rejected.
(The remaining
p
-values are shown in Figure
8.3
in the Appendix.)
Notably, in each row, one distribution is always tightly concentrated at
-\log_{10}p=0
(i.e.
p=1
).
This means that, for all physiological outcomes of interest, there was either very little evidence in favour of rejecting any
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lo}}
, or very little in favour of rejecting any
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{up}}
.
In other words, across configurations of
(t,f,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
that were rejected, the twin consistently either underestimated or overestimated each quantity on average.
For example, Pulse consistently underestimated chloride blood concentration and skin temperature, while it consistently overestimated sodium and glucose blood concentration levels.
Like Table
4.1
, this information appears of interest and actionable in a variety of downstream tasks.

Figure 4.1
:
Distributions of
-\log_{10}{p_{\textup{lo}}}
and
-\log_{10}{p_{\textup{up}}}
across hypotheses, grouped by physiological quantity. Higher values indicate greater evidence in favour of rejection.

4.6.4
Pitfalls of naive assessment

A naive approach to twin assessment involves simply comparing the output of the twin with the observational data directly, without accounting for causal considerations.
We now show that, unlike our methodology, the results produced in this way can be potentially misleading.
In Figure
4.2
, for two different choices of
(a_{1:4},B_{1:4})
, we plot estimates of
\widehat{Q}_{t}
and
Q^{\textup{obs}}_{t}
for
t\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
, where

\displaystyle\widehat{Q}_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[\widehat{Y}(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0}\in B_{0},\widehat{X}_{1:t}(X_{0},a_{1:t})\in B_{1:t}]

\displaystyle Q^{\textup{obs}}_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[Y(A_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(A_{1:t})\in B_{0:t},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}].

Figure 4.2
:
Estimates and 95% confidence intervals for
\widehat{Q}_{t}
and
Q^{\textup{obs}}_{t}
at each
1\leq t\leq 4
for two choices of
(B_{0:4},a_{1:4})
, where
\widehat{Y}(a_{1:t})
and
Y(a_{1:t})
correspond to HCO
3
concentration.
The dashed lines indicate lower and upper 95% confidence intervals for
Q_{\mathrm{lo}},Q_{\mathrm{up}}
respectively.

Here
\widehat{Q}_{t}
is just
\widehat{Q}
as defined above with its dependence on
t
made explicit.
Each plot also shows one-sided 95% confidence intervals on
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
Q_{\mathrm{up}}
at each
t\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
obtained from Hoeffding’s inequality.
Directly comparing the estimates of
\widehat{Q}_{t}
and
Q^{\textup{obs}}_{t}
would suggest that the twin is comparatively more accurate for the right-hand plot, as these estimates are closer to one another in that case.
However, the output of the twin in the right-hand plot is falsified at
t=1
, as can be seen from the fact that confidence interval for
\widehat{Q}_{1}
lies entirely above the one-sided confidence interval for
Q_{\mathrm{up}}
at that timestep.
On the other hand, the output of the twin in the left-hand plot is not falsified at any of the timesteps shown, so that the twin may in fact be accurate for these
(a_{1:4},B_{1:4})
, contrary to what a naive assessment strategy would suggest.
Our methodology provides a principled means for twin assessment that avoids drawing potentially misleading inferences like this.

A similar phenomenon appears in Figure
4.3
, which for two choices of
B_{0:t}
and
a_{1:t}
shows histograms of raw glucose values obtained from the observational data conditional on
A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}
and
X_{0:t}(A_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}
, and from the twin conditional on
\widehat{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}
.
Below each histogram we also show 95% confidence intervals for
Q_{\mathrm{up}}
and
\widehat{Q}
obtained from Hoeffding’s inequality.
While Figures
4.3(a)
and
4.3(b)
appear visually very similar, the inferences produced by our testing procedure are different: the hypothesis corresponding to the right-hand plot is rejected, since there is no overlap between the confidence intervals underneath, while the hypothesis corresponding to the left-hand plot is not.
This was not an isolated case and several other examples of this phenomenon are shown in Figure
8.4
in the Appendix.
This demonstrates that the inferences obtained from our procedure do not depend only on the distribution of observed outcomes (which is essentially the same for both cases).
Instead, as discussed in Section
4.4.1
, these also account for the worst-case effects of unmeasured confounding that may exist in the observational data.

(a)

Not rejected

(b)

Rejected

Figure 4.3
:
Raw glucose values from the observational data and twin for two choices of
(B_{0:t},a_{1:t})
, with confidence intervals for
\widehat{Q}
and
Q_{\mathrm{up}}
shown below. The horizontal axes are truncated to the .025 and .975 quantiles of the observational data for clarity. Untruncated plots are shown in Figure
8.4
of the Appendix.

4.7
Discussion

We have advocated for a causal approach to digital twin assessment, and have presented a statistical procedure for doing so that obtains rigorous theoretical guarantees under minimal assumptions.
We now highlight the key limitations of our approach.
Importantly, our methodology implicitly assumes that there is no distribution shift between testing and deployment time.
If the conditional distribution of
X_{1:T}(a_{1:T})
given
X_{0}
changes at deployment time, then so too does the set of twins that are interventionally correct, and if this change is significant enough, our assessment procedure may yield misleading results.
Distribution shift in this sense is a separate issue to unobserved confounding, and arises in a wide variety of statistical problems beyond ours.

Additionally, the procedure we used in our case study to choose the hypothesis parameters
B_{0:t}
was ad hoc.
For scalability, it would likely be necessary to obtain
B_{0:t}
via a more automated procedure.
It may also be desirable to choose
B_{0:t}
dynamically in light of previous hypotheses tested, zooming in to regions containing possible failure modes to obtain increasingly granular information about the twin.
We see opportunities here for using machine learning techniques, but leave this to future work.

Various other extensions and improvements appear possible.
For example,
one can leverage ideas from the literature on partial identification
(
Manski 2003
)
to obtain greater statistical efficiency, for example by building on the line of work initiated by
Imbens and Manski 2004
for obtaining more informative confidence intervals.
Beyond this, it may sometimes be useful to consider additional assumptions that lead to less conservative assessment results.
For example, various methods for
sensitivity analysis
have been proposed that model the
degree
to which the actions of the behavioural agent are confounded
(
Rosenbaum 2002
;
Tan 2006
;
Yadlowsky et al. 2022
)
.
This can yield tighter bounds on
Q
than are implied by Theorem
4.4.1
, albeit at the expense of less robustness if these assumptions are violated.

Chapter 5
Conclusion and Future Work

\minitoc

5.1
Discussion

Before deploying a decision-making policy to production, it is usually important to understand the
plausible range of outcomes that it may produce. However, due to resource or ethical constraints, it is
often not possible to obtain this understanding by testing the policy directly in the real-world. In such
cases we have to rely on off-policy evaluation (OPE), which uses observational data collected under a different behavioural policy to evaluate the target policy in some way.
In this thesis, we have considered some of the challenges posed by the current OPE methodologies and proposed novel solutions to each of these individually.
We have also demonstrated the practical utility of these solutions by applying them to a range of real-world problems involving large scale datasets.
To be more specific, below we provide a brief summary of the challenges tackled in this thesis:

•

In Chapter
2
we consider the problem of variance reduction in OPE estimators. To address this, we propose the Marginal Ratio (MR) estimator, which uses a marginalization technique to provide a more efficient and robust estimator for contextual bandits, and may also be of interest in other domains such as causal inference.

•

Next, in Chapter
3
we provide a novel methodology of uncertainty quantification in off-policy outcomes based on conformal prediction
(
Vovk et al. 2005
)
. Our proposed technique can help practitioners quantify the plausible range of outcomes that are likely to occur under the target policy, and comes with sound finite-sample probabilistic guarantees.

•

Finally, in Chapter
4
we explore the case when the assumption of no unmeasured confounding (needed for the existing OPE methodologies) is violated.
We provide a set of novel causal bounds which remain valid in this case, and subsequently use these bounds to develop a procedure for robust assessment of digital twin models using observational data which remains valid under only the assumption of an i.i.d. dataset of observational trajectories.

5.2
Limitations

Here, we outline some of the limitations of the methodologies described in this thesis.

Distributional shift in data generating mechanism

Our methodologies highlighted in this thesis implicitly assume that the data generating process remains unchanged between testing and deployment times.
Technically, for contextual bandits this assumption means that the conditional distribution of
Y
given
(X,A)
does not shift when the target policy is deployed.
If this distribution changes at deployment time, then so too does the distribution of outcomes that
would
be observed under the target policy.
If this shift is significant enough, our methodologies may yield misleading results.

Estimation errors

The techniques mentioned in Chapters
2
and
3
involve an additional estimation of marginal density ratios for importance sampling.
While we outline straightforward regression methodologies for estimating these importance ratios directly, this step may still introduce an additional source of bias in the value estimation.

Scalability limitations

Increasing data dimensionality may pose additional challenges for our solutions, especially those described in Chapters
3
and
4
.
For example, in Chapter
3
, the estimation of importance ratios for our conformal off-policy prediction (COPP) algorithm may become more challenging when
(X,A)
is high-dimensional, thereby yielding biased results.
Likewise in Chapter
4
, the procedure we used in our case study to choose the hypothesis parameters was ad hoc, which may not scale to high-dimensional datasets.
For scalability, it would likely be necessary to obtain these parameters via a more automated procedure.

5.3
Directions for future work

Our work in this thesis opens up several interesting avenues for future research. We highlight some of these below.

Off-policy learning

This thesis has largely focused on robust off-policy assessment methodologies. However, our findings are highly applicable to robust policy optimisation problems, where the data collected using an ‘old’ policy is used to learn a new policy.
Proximal Policy Optimisation
(
Schulman et al. 2017
)
is one such approach which has gained immense popularity and has been applied to reinforcement learning with human feedback
(
Lambert et al. 2022
)
.
We believe that our MR estimator proposed in Chapter
2
applied to these methodologies could lead to improvements in the stability and convergence
of these optimisation schemes, given its favourable variance properties.
Similarly, our conformal off-policy prediction (COPP) algorithm when applied to off-policy learning could be a
step towards robust policy learning by optimising the worst-case outcome
(
Stutz et al. 2022
)
.

Addressing the curse of horizon in sequential decision-making

Chapters
2
and
3
of this thesis specifically consider OPE in contextual bandits.
This setting offers a strong foundational framework for conducting rigorous theoretical and empirical analyses,
however, it would be interesting to extend the application of these methodologies to sequential decision frameworks.
While some follow-up works have attempted to apply our COPP algorithm to Markov Decision Processes
(
Foffano et al. 2023
;
Zhang et al. 2023
;
Kuipers et al. 2024
)
, the obtained confidence sets become increasingly conservative with increasing time horizon.
It is worth exploring methodologies for obtaining intervals which remain valid and informative even in sequential decision settings with large time horizons.

Application to transfer learning

Finally, our solutions in Chapters
2
and
3
involves learning importance ratios which may also be of interest in other domains beyond OPE.
One such area is
transfer learning
which considers cases where the testing data distribution is different from the training data distribution.
Classical transfer learning methods rely on importance weighting to handle the distribution mismatch
(
Shimodaira 2000
;
Sugiyama et al. 2007
;
Huang et al. 2007
;
Sugiyama et al. 2008
;
Lu et al. 2021
)
.
Our proposed regression techniques may be of interest for obtaining these weights efficiently in high-dimensional datasets in this setting.

\startappendices

Chapter 6
Marginal Density Ratio for Off-Policy
Evaluation in Contextual Bandits

\minitoc

6.1
Proofs

Proof 6.1.1

(Proof of Lemma
2.3.1
)
.

First, we express the weights
w(y)
as the conditional expectation as follows:

\displaystyle w(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(y)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(x,a,y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(y)}\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(x,a,y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(y)}\,\frac{p_{\pi^{b}}(x,a\mid y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(x,a\mid y)}\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(x,a,y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(x,a,y)}\,p_{\pi^{b}}(x,a\mid y)\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}}\rho(a,x)\,p_{\pi^{b}}(x,a\mid y)\,\mathrm{d}a\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y=y],

where
\rho(a,x)=\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(x,a,y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(x,a,y)}=\frac{\pi^{*}(a\mid x)}{\pi^{b}(a\mid x)}
.
Since conditional expectations can be defined as the solution of regression problem, the result follows.

Proof 6.1.2

(Proof of Proposition
2.3.2
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\textup{D}_{f}\left(p_{\pi^{*}}(x,a,y)\,||\,p_{\pi^{b}}(x,a,y)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[f\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(X,A,Y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(X,A,Y)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[f\left(\frac{\pi^{*}(A\mid X)}{\pi^{b}(A\mid X)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[f\left(\frac{\pi^{*}(A\mid X)}{\pi^{b}(A\mid X)}\right)\Bigg|Y\right]\right]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[f\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A\mid X)}{\pi^{b}(A\mid X)}\Bigg|Y\right]\right)\right]\quad\text{(Jensen's inequality)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[f\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(Y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(Y)}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\textup{D}_{f}\left(p_{\pi^{*}}(y)\,||\,p_{\pi^{b}}(y)\right).

Proof 6.1.3

(Proof of Proposition
2.3.3
)
.

Since
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y]
, we have that,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)^{2}\,Y^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[w(Y)^{2}\,Y^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\mid Y]\,Y^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[w(Y)^{2}\,Y^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\mid Y]\,Y^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y]^{2}\,Y^{2}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\mid Y\right]\,Y^{2}\right].

In the second last step above, we use the fact that
w(y)=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y=y]
.

Proof 6.1.4

(Proof of Proposition
2.3.4
)
.

Let
\hat{\mu}(a,x)\approx\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x,A=a]
denote the outcome model in DR estimator. Then, using multiple applications of the law of total variance we get that

\displaystyle n\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\hat{\mu}(a^{\prime},X)\,\pi^{*}(a^{\prime}\mid X)\right]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\quad+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\quad+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\quad+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\quad+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]\mid X]]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]\mid X]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))\mid X]]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]].

Using this, we get that

\displaystyle n(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}])

\displaystyle\quad\geq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]\right]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[w(Y)\,Y].

Again, using the law of total variance,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,Y]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,Y\mid X,A]]+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,Y\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)^{2}\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]\right].

Rearranging and substituting back into the expression earlier, we get that

\displaystyle n(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}])

\displaystyle\quad\geq\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,Y]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]\right]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[w(Y)\,Y].

Now, from Proposition
2.3.3
we know that

\displaystyle n(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}])=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,Y]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[w(Y)\,Y]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\mid Y\right]\,Y^{2}\right].

Therefore,

\displaystyle n(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}])

\displaystyle\quad\geq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\mid Y\right]\,Y^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\,Y\mid Y\right]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]\right].

Proof 6.1.5

(Proof of Theorem
2.3.6
)
.

This result follows straightforwardly from Proposition
6.4.5
in Appendix
6.4
.

Proof 6.1.6

(Proof of Proposition
2.3.7
)
.

\displaystyle\textup{Bias}(\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\,Y]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{w}(Y)\,Y]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y]

\displaystyle=\textup{Bias}(\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}})-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y].

Next, to prove the variance result, we first use the law of total variance to obtain

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\,Y]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\,Y\mid Y]]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\,Y\mid Y]]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\,Y\mid Y]]\right).

Moreover, using the fact that
\hat{w}(Y)=\tilde{w}(Y)+\epsilon
we get that,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{w}(Y)\,Y]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\left(\tilde{w}(Y)+\epsilon\right)\,Y]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y]+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]+2\,\textup{Cov}(\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y,\epsilon\,Y)\right).

Putting together the two variance expressions derived above, we get that

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{n}\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]-2\,\textup{Cov}(\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y,\epsilon\,Y)\right).

6.2
Comparison with extensions of the doubly robust estimator

In this section, we theoretically investigate the variance of MR against the commonly used extensions of the DR estimator, namely Switch-DR
(
Wang et al. 2017b
)
and DR with Optimistic Shrinkage (DRos)
(
Su et al. 2020
)
. At a high level, these estimators seek to reduce the variance of the vanilla DR estimator by considering modified importance weights, thereby trading off the variance for additional bias.
Below, we provide the explicit definitions of these estimators for completeness.

Switch-DR estimator

The original DR estimator can still have a high variance when the importance weights are large due to a large policy shift. Switch-DR
(
Wang et al. 2017b
)
aims to circumvent this problem by switching to DM when the importance weights are large:

\hat{\theta}_{\textup{SwitchDR}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho(a_{i},x_{i})\,(y_{i}-\hat{\mu}(a_{i},x_{i}))\mathbbm{1}(\rho(a_{i},x_{i})\leq\tau)+\hat{\eta}(\pi^{*}),

where
\tau\geq 0
is a hyperparameter,
\hat{\mu}(a,x)\approx\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x,A=a]
is the outcome model, and

\hat{\eta}(\pi^{*})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\hat{\mu}(a^{\prime},x_{i})\pi^{*}(a^{\prime}\mid x_{i})\approx\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\hat{\mu}(A,X)]

where
a_{i}^{*}\sim\pi^{*}(\cdot\mid x_{i})
.

Doubly Robust with Optimal Shrinkage (DRos)

DRos proposed by
(
Su et al. 2020
)
uses new weights
\hat{\rho}_{\lambda}(a_{i},x_{i})
which directly minimises sharp bounds on the MSE of the resulting estimator,

\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DRos}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\rho}_{\lambda}(a_{i},x_{i})\,(y_{i}-\hat{\mu}(a_{i},x_{i}))+\hat{\eta}(\pi^{*}),

where
\lambda\geq 0
is a pre-defined hyperparameter and
\hat{\rho}_{\lambda}
is defined as

\hat{\rho}_{\lambda}(a,x)\coloneqq\frac{\lambda}{\rho^{2}(a,x)+\lambda}\,\rho(a,x).

When
\lambda=0
,
\hat{\rho}_{\lambda}(a,x)=0
leads to DM, whereas as
\lambda\rightarrow\infty
,
\hat{\rho}_{\lambda}(a,x)\rightarrow\rho(a,x)
leading to DR.

More generally, both of these estimators can be written as follows:

\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}^{\tilde{\rho}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\rho}(a_{i},x_{i})\,(y_{i}-\hat{\mu}(a_{i},x_{i}))+\hat{\eta}(\pi^{*}).

Here, when
\tilde{\rho}(a,x)=\rho(a,x)\mathbbm{1}(\rho(a_{i},x_{i})\leq\tau)
, we recover the Switch-DR estimator and likewise when
\tilde{\rho}(a,x)=\hat{\rho}_{\lambda}(a,x)
, we recover DRos.

6.2.1
Variance comparison with the DR extensions

Next, we provide a theoretical result comparing the variance of the MR estimator with these DR extension methods.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

When the weights
w(y)
are known exactly and the outcome model is exact, i.e.,
\hat{\mu}(a,x)=\mu(a,x)=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x,A=a]
in the DR estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}^{\tilde{\rho}}
defined above,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}^{\tilde{\rho}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\geq\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\mid Y\right]\,Y^{2}-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\mu(A,X)\mid X\right]\right]-\Delta,

where
\Delta\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[(\rho^{2}(A,X)-\tilde{\rho}^{2}(A,X))\,\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]\right]
.

Proof 6.2.2

(Proof of Proposition
6.2.1
)
.

Using the fact that
\hat{\mu}(a,x)=\mu(a,x)
and the law of total variance, we get that

\displaystyle n\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}^{\tilde{\rho}}]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\rho}(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\hat{\mu}(a^{\prime},X)\pi^{*}(a^{\prime}\mid X)]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\rho}(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\rho}(A,X)\,(Y-\mu(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\mu(A,X)\mid X]]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\rho}(A,X)\,(Y-\mu(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\mu(A,X)\mid X]\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\rho}(A,X)\,(Y-\mu(A,X))+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\mu(A,X)\mid X]\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\rho}^{2}(A,X)\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho^{2}(A,X)\,\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\qquad+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[(\tilde{\rho}^{2}(A,X)-\rho^{2}(A,X))\,\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]}_{-n\,\Delta}

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho^{2}(A,X)\,\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]-n\,\Delta.

Again, using the law of total variance we can rewrite the second term on the RHS above as,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho^{2}(A,X)\,\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,Y]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[w(Y)\,Y]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)]

\displaystyle\quad=n\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)].

Putting this together, we get that

\displaystyle n\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{DR}}^{\tilde{\rho}}]

\displaystyle\quad=n\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)]

\displaystyle\qquad+\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]-n\,\Delta

\displaystyle\quad=n\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]-n\,\Delta,

where in the last step above, we again use the law of total variance. Rearranging the above leads us to the result.

Intuition

Note that for both of the DR extensions under consideration, the modified ratios
\tilde{\rho}(a,x)
satisfy
0\leq\tilde{\rho}(a,x)\leq\rho(a,x)
and hence
\Delta\geq 0
(using the definition of
\Delta
in Proposition
6.2.1
).
When the modified ratios
\tilde{\rho}(a,x)
are ‘close’ to the true policy ratios
\rho(a,x)
, then using the definition of
\Delta
, we have that
\Delta\approx 0
. In this case, the result above provides a similar intuition to Proposition
2.3.4
in the main text. Specifically, in this case we have that if
\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\,Y\mid Y\right]
is greater than
\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X\right]
on average, the variance of the MR estimator will be less than that of the DR extension under consideration.
Intuitively, this will occur when the dimension of context space
\mathcal{X}
is high because in this case the conditional variance over
X
and
A
,
\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\,Y\mid Y\right]
is likely to be greater than the conditional variance over
A
,
\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\rho(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X\right]
.

In contrast if the modified ratios
\tilde{\rho}(a,x)
differ substantially from
\rho(a,x)
, then
\Delta
will be large and the variance of MR may be higher than that of the resulting DR extension. However, this comes at the cost of significantly higher bias in the DR extension and consequently MSE of the DR extension will be high in this case.

6.3
Weight estimation error

In this section, we theoretically investigate the effects of using the estimated importance weights
\hat{w}(y)
rather than
\hat{\rho}(a,x)
on the bias and variance of the resulting OPE estimator. Further to our discussion in Section
2.3.1
, we focus in this section on the approximation error when using a wide neural network to estimate the weights
\hat{w}(y)
. To this end, we use recent results regarding the generalization of wide neural networks
(
Lai et al. 2023
)
to show that the estimation error of the approximation step (ii) in the Section
2.3.1
declines with increasing number of training data when
\hat{w}(y)
is estimated using wide neural networks. Before providing the main result, we explicitly lay out the assumptions needed.

6.3.1
Using wide neural networks to approximate the weights
\hat{w}(y)

Assumption 6.3.1
.

Let

\tilde{w}(y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\mid Y=y].

Suppose
\tilde{w}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
||\tilde{w}||_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\leq R
for some constant
R
, where
\mathcal{H}_{1}
is the reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) associated with the Neural Tangent Kernel
K_{1}
associated with 2 layer neural network defined on
\mathbb{R}
.

Assumption 6.3.2
.

There exists an
M\in[0,\infty)
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\pi^{b}}(|Y|\leq M)=1
.

Assumption 6.3.3
.

\hat{\rho}(a_{i},x_{i})
satisfies

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}(a_{i},x_{i})=\tilde{w}(y_{i})+\eta_{i},

where
\eta_{i}\overset{\textup{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
for some
\sigma>0
.

Theorem 6.3.4
.

Suppose that the IPW and MR estimators are defined as,

\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\rho}(a_{i},x_{i})\,y_{i},\quad\text{and }\quad\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{m}(y_{i})\,y_{i},

where
\hat{w}_{m}(y)
is obtained by regressing to the estimated policy ratios
\hat{\rho}(a,x)
using
m
i.i.d. training samples
\mathcal{D}_{\textup{tr}}\coloneqq\{(x^{\textup{tr}}_{i},a^{\textup{tr}}_{i},y^{\textup{tr}}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}
, i.e., by minimising the loss

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\phi)=\mathbb{E}_{(X,A,Y)\sim\mathcal{D}_{\textup{tr}}}\left[\left(\hat{\rho}(A,X)-f_{\phi}(Y)\right)^{2}\right].

Suppose Assumptions
6.3.1
-
6.3.3
hold, then for any given
\delta\in(0,1)
, if
f_{\phi}
is a two-layer neural network with width
k
that is sufficiently large and stops the gradient flow at time
t_{*}\propto m^{2/3}
, then for sufficiently large
m
, there exists a constant
C_{1}
independent of
\delta
and
m
, such that

|\textup{Bias}(\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{MR}})-\textup{Bias}(\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}})|\leq C_{1}\,m^{-1/3}\log{\frac{6}{\delta}}

holds with probability at least
(1-\delta)(1-o_{k}(1))
. Moreover,
there exist constants
C_{2},C_{3}
independent of
\delta
and
m
such that

\displaystyle n(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}])

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\geq\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\,Y\mid Y]]}_{\geq 0}-C_{2}\,m^{-2/3}\,\log^{2}{\frac{6}{\delta}}-C_{3}\,m^{-1/3}\,\log{\frac{6}{\delta}}

holds with probability at least
(1-\delta)(1-o_{k}(1))
. Here, the randomness comes from the joint distribution of training samples and random initialization of parameters in the neural network
f_{\phi}
.

Proof 6.3.5

(Proof of Theorem
6.3.4
)
.

The proof of this theorem relies on
(
Lai et al. 2023
, Theorem 4.1)
.
Recall the definition
\tilde{w}(Y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\mid Y]
.
We can rewrite our setup in the setting of
(
Lai et al. 2023
, Theorem 4.1)
, by relabelling
\hat{\rho}(a,x)
in our setup as
y
in their setup and relabelling
y
in our setup as
x
in their setup.
Then, given
\delta\in(0,1)
, from
(
Lai et al. 2023
, Theorem 4.1)
, it follows that under Assumptions
6.3.1
-
6.3.3
that there exists a constant
C
independent of
\delta
and
m
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon^{2}]\leq C\,m^{-2/3}\,\log^{2}{\frac{6}{\delta}}

(6.1)

holds with probability at least
(1-\delta)(1-o_{k}(1))
, where
\epsilon\coloneqq\hat{w}_{m}(Y)-\tilde{w}(Y)
. Recall from Proposition
2.3.7
that

|\textup{Bias}(\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{MR}})-\textup{Bias}(\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}})|=|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]|.

From this it follows using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that,

\displaystyle|\textup{Bias}(\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{MR}})-\textup{Bias}(\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}})|

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]|\leq\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y^{2}]\right)^{1/2}.

Combining the above with Eqn. (
6.1
), it follows that,

\displaystyle|\textup{Bias}(\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{MR}})-\textup{Bias}(\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}})|\leq C^{1/2}\,m^{-1/3}\,\log{\frac{6}{\delta}}(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y^{2}])^{1/2}=C_{1}\,m^{-1/3}\,\log{\frac{6}{\delta}}

holds with probability at least
(1-\delta)(1-o_{k}(1))
, where
C_{1}=C^{1/2}\,(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y^{2}])^{1/2}
.

Next, to prove the variance result, we recall from Proposition
2.3.7
that

\displaystyle n(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}])

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]-2\,\textup{Cov}(\epsilon\,Y,\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y)

Now note that, under Assumption
6.3.2
,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[(\epsilon\,Y)^{2}]\leq M^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon^{2}]\leq C\,M^{2}\,m^{-2/3}\,\log^{2}{\frac{6}{\delta}}=C_{2}\,m^{-2/3}\,\log^{2}{\frac{6}{\delta}},

holds with probability at least
(1-\delta)(1-o_{k}(1))
, where
C_{2}=C\,M^{2}
. Similarly, we have that with probability at least
(1-\delta)(1-o_{k}(1))
,

\displaystyle|\textup{Cov}(\epsilon\,Y,\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y)|

\displaystyle=|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y^{2}]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y]|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y^{2}]|+|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon\,Y]\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y]|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)^{2}\,Y^{4}]\right)^{1/2}+(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon^{2}]\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y^{2}])^{1/2}|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y]|

\displaystyle=(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\epsilon^{2}])^{1/2}\,\left((\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)^{2}\,Y^{4}])^{1/2}+(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y^{2}])^{1/2}\,|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y]|\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{3}\,m^{-1/3}\,\log{\frac{6}{\delta}},

where
C_{3}=C\,(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)^{2}\,Y^{4}])^{1/2}+(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y^{2}])^{1/2}\,|\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\tilde{w}(Y)\,Y]|
, and we use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in the third step above. Putting this together, we obtain the required result.

Intuition

This theorem shows that as the number of training samples
m
increases, the biases of MR and IPW estimators become roughly equal, whereas the variance of MR estimator falls below that of the IPW estimator. The empirical results shown in Appendix
6.6.2
are consistent with this result.
Moreover, in Theorem
6.3.4
, the estimated policy ratio
\hat{\rho}(a,x)
is fixed for increasing
m
, i.e., we do not update
\hat{\rho}(a,x)
as more training data becomes available. While this may seem as a disadvantage for the IPW estimator, we point out that the result also holds when the policy ratio is exact (i.e.,
\hat{\rho}(a,x)=\rho(a,x)
) and hence the IPW estimator is unbiased.

Relaxing Assumption
6.3.3

Lai et al. 2023
[Theorem 4.1] suppose that the data has the relationship shown in Assumption
6.3.3
. However, the theorem relies on Corollary 4.4 in
Lin et al. 2020
, which requires a strictly weaker assumption (Assumption 1 in
Lin et al. 2020
). Therefore, we can relax Assumption
6.3.3
to the following assumption.

Assumption 6.3.6
.

There exists positive constants
Q
and
M
such that for all
l\geq 2
with
l\in\mathbb{N}

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)^{l}\mid Y]\leq\frac{1}{2}\,l!\,M^{l-2}\,Q^{2}

p_{\pi^{b}}
-almost surely.

It is easy to check that Assumption
6.3.6
is strictly weaker than Assumption
6.3.3
, and is also satisfied if the policy ratio
\hat{\rho}(A,X)
is almost surely bounded. For simplicity, we use the stronger assumption in our Proposition
6.3.4
.

6.4
Generalised formulation of the MIPS estimator
(
Saito and Joachims 2022
)

As described in Section
2.3.1
, the MIPS estimator proposed by
Saito and Joachims 2022
assumes the existence of
action embeddings

E
which summarise all relevant information about the action
A
, and achieves a lower variance than the IPW estimator. To achieve this, the MIPS estimator only considers the shift in the distribution of
(X,E)
as a result of policy shift, instead of considering the shift in
(X,A)
(as in IPW estimator). In this section, we show that this idea can be generalised to instead consider general representations
R
of the context-action pair
(X,A)
, which encapsulate all relevant information about the outcome
Y
. The MIPS estimator is a special case of this generalised setting where the representation
R
is of the form
(X,E)
.

Generalised MIPS (G-MIPS) estimator

Suppose that there exists an embedding
R
of the context-action pair
(X,A)
, with the Bayesian network shown in Figure
6.1
. Here,
R
may be a lower-dimensional representation of the
(X,A)
pair which contains all the information necessary to predict the outcome
Y
. This corresponds to the following conditional independence assumption:

Assumption 6.4.1
.

The context-action pair
(X,A)
has no direct effect on the outcome
Y
given
R
, i.e.,

Y\perp\!\!\!\perp(X,A)\mid R
.

(X,A)

R
Y

Figure 6.1
:
Bayesian network corresponding to Assumption
6.4.1
.

As illustrated in Figure
6.1
, Assumption
6.4.1
means that the embedding
R
fully mediates every possible effect of
(X,A)
on
Y
. The generalised MIPS estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}
of target policy value,
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y]
, is defined as

\hat{\theta}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r_{i})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r_{i})}\,y_{i},

where
p_{\pi^{b}}(r)
denote the density of
R
under the behaviour policy (likewise for
p_{\pi^{*}}(r)
). Under assumption
6.4.1
,
\hat{\theta}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}
provides an unbiased estimator of target policy value.
Similar to Lemma
2.3.1
, the density ratio
\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r)}
can be estimated by solving the regression problem

\displaystyle\arg\min_{f}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left(\frac{\pi^{*}(A\mid X)}{\pi^{b}(A\mid X)}-f\left(R\right)\right)^{2}.

(6.2)

6.4.1
Variance reduction of G-MIPS estimator

By only considering the shift in the embedding
R
, the G-MIPS estimator achieves a lower variance relative to the vanilla IPW estimator. The following result, which is a straightforward extension of
(
Saito and Joachims 2022
, Theorem 3.6)
, formalises this.

Proposition 6.4.2

(Variance reduction of G-MIPS)
.

When the ratios
\rho(a,x)
and
\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r)}
are known exactly then under Assumption
6.4.1
, we have that
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y]
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]\geq\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}\mid R]\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\rho(A,X)\mid R]\right]\geq 0.

Proof 6.4.3

(Proof of Proposition
6.4.2
)
.

The following proof, which is included for completeness, is a straightforward extension of
(
Saito and Joachims 2022
, Theorem 3.6)
.

\displaystyle n(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MIPS}}])

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\,Y\right]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\,Y\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right]

Now using the conditional independence Assumption
6.4.1
, the first term on the RHS above becomes,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\Bigg|R\right]\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[Y|R\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[Y|R\right]\right],

where in the last step above we use the fact that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\Bigg|R\right]=\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}.

Putting this together, we get that

\displaystyle n(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MIPS}}])

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right].

(6.3)

Since we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\Bigg|R\right]\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[Y|R\right]=\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[Y|R\right],

Eq. (
6.3
) becomes,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\,Y\Bigg|R\right]\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\left(\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\,Y\right)^{2}\Bigg|R\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\,Y\right)^{2}\Bigg|R\right]\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\left(\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\right)^{2}\Bigg|R\right]\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[Y^{2}|R\right]-\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R)}\right)^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[Y^{2}|R\right]\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[Y^{2}|R\right]\,\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\left(\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\right)^{2}\Bigg|R\right]-\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\Bigg|R\right]\right)^{2}\,\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[Y^{2}|R\right]\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}\Bigg|R\right]\right].

Intuition

Here,
R
contains all relevant information regarding the outcome
Y
. Moreover, intuitively
R
can be thought of as the state obtained by ‘filtering out’ relevant information about
Y
from
(X,A)
. Therefore,
R
contains less ‘redundant’ information regarding the outcome
Y
as compared to the covariate-action pair
(X,A)
. As a result, the G-MIPS estimator which only considers the shift in the marginal distribution of
R
due to the policy shift is more efficient than the IPW estimator, which considers the shift in the joint distribution of
(X,A)
instead.
In fact, as the amount of ‘redundant’ information regarding
Y
decreases in the embedding
R
, the G-MIPS estimator becomes increasingly efficient with decreasing variance. We formalise this as follows:

Assumption 6.4.4
.

Assume there exist embeddings
R^{(1)},R^{(2)}
of the covariate-action pair
(X,A)
, with Bayesian network shown in Figure
6.2
.
This corresponds to the following conditional independence assumptions:

R^{(2)}\perp\!\!\!\perp(X,A)\mid R^{(1)},\qquad\textup{and}\qquad Y\perp\!\!\!\perp(R^{(1)},X,A)\mid R^{(2)}.

(X,A)
R^{(1)}
R^{(2)}
Y

Figure 6.2
:
Bayesian network corresponding to Assumption
6.4.4
.

We can define G-MIPS estimators for these embeddings to obtain unbiased OPE estimators under Assumption
6.4.4
as follows:

\hat{\theta}^{(j)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r_{i}^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r_{i}^{(j)})}\,y_{i},

for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Here,
\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)})}
is the ratio of marginal densities of
R^{(j)}
under target and behaviour policies.
We next show that the variance of
\hat{\theta}^{(j)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}
decreases with increasing
j
.

Proposition 6.4.5
.

When the ratios
\rho(a,x)
,
w(y)
and
\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)})}
are known exactly for
j\in\{1,2\}
, then under Assumption
6.4.4
we get that

\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(1)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(2)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y].

Moreover,

\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}]\geq\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(1)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]\geq\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(2)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]\geq\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}].

Proof 6.4.6

(Proof of Proposition
6.4.5
)
.

First, we prove that the G-MIPS estimators are unbiased using induction on
j
. We define
R^{(0)}\coloneqq(X,A)
and
\hat{\theta}^{(0)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}
defined as

\hat{\theta}^{(0)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r_{i}^{(0)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r_{i}^{(0)})}\,y_{i},

recovers the IPW estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}
. When
j=0
, we know that
\hat{\theta}^{(0)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}=\hat{\theta}_{\textup{IPW}}
is unbiased.
Now, assume that
\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(j)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y]
.

Conditional on
R^{(j)}
,
R^{(j+1)}
does not depend on the policy. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)})}=\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{(j)})\,p(r^{(j+1)}\mid r^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)})\,p(r^{(j+1)}\mid r^{(j)})}=\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{(j)},r^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)},r^{(j+1)})}.

And therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j+1)})}

\displaystyle=\int_{r^{(j)}}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{(j)},r^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)},r^{(j+1)})}\,p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)}\mid r^{(j+1)})\,\mathrm{d}r^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\int_{r^{(j)}}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)})}\,p_{\pi^{b}}(r^{(j)}\mid r^{(j+1)})\,\mathrm{d}r^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}=r^{(j+1)}\right].

Using this and the fact that
R^{(j)}\perp\!\!\!\perp Y\mid R^{(j+1)}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\hat{\theta}^{(j+1)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\,Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y|R^{(j+1)}]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y|R^{(j+1)}]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\,Y\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\,Y\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\hat{\theta}^{(j)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{*}}[Y].

Next, to prove the variance result we consider the difference

\displaystyle\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(j)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(j+1)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\,Y\right]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\,Y\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\Bigg(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\,Y\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\,Y\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y\mid R^{(j+1)}]\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\right)^{2}\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[Y\mid R^{(j+1)}]\right]\Bigg)

where in the last step we use the law of total variance. Now, using the fact that
R^{(j)}\perp\!\!\!\perp Y\mid R^{(j+1)}
, we can rewrite the expression above as

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\Bigg(\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y|R^{(j+1)}]\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\,Y\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y\mid R^{(j+1)}]\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\right)^{2}\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[Y\mid R^{(j+1)}]\right]\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\Bigg(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\,Y\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\right)^{2}\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[Y\mid R^{(j+1)}]\right]\Bigg).

Moreover, again using the conditional independence
R^{(j)}\perp\!\!\!\perp Y\mid R^{(j+1)}
, we can expand the first term in the expression above as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\,Y\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\Bigg[\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}^{2}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}^{2}(R^{(j)})}\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y^{2}|R^{(j+1)}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y|R^{(j+1)}]\right)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\Bigg[\left(\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j)})}\Bigg|R^{(j+1)}\right]\right)^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y^{2}|R^{(j+1)}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}[Y|R^{(j+1)}]\right)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\Bigg[\left(\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(R^{(j+1)})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(R^{(j+1)})}\right)^{2}\,\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[Y\mid R^{(j+1)}]\Bigg].

Here, to get the inequality above, we use the fact that
\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]\geq(\mathbb{E}[X])^{2}
. Putting this together, we get that
\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(j)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]-\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(j+1)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]\geq 0
.

Moreover, the result
\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(2)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]\geq\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}]
follows straightforwardly from above by defining
R^{(3)}\coloneqq Y
. Then, the embeddings satisfy the causal structure

R^{(0)}\rightarrow R^{(1)}\rightarrow R^{(2)}\rightarrow R^{(3)}\rightarrow Y.

Using the result above, we know that
\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(2)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]\geq\textup{Var}_{\pi^{b}}[\hat{\theta}^{(3)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}]
. But now it is straightforward to see that
\hat{\theta}^{(3)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}=\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}
, and the result follows.

Intuition

Here,
R^{(j+1)}
can be thought of as the embedding obtained by ‘filtering out’ relevant information about
Y
from
R^{(j)}
. As such, the amount of ‘redundant’ information regarding the outcome
Y
decreases successively along the sequence
R^{(0)}(\coloneqq(X,A)),R^{(1)},R^{(2)}
. As a result, the G-MIPS estimators which only consider the shift in the marginal distributions of
R^{(j)}
due to policy shift become increasingly efficient with decreasing variance as
j
increases. Define the representation
R^{(3)}\coloneqq Y
, then the corresponding G-MIPS estimator reduces to the MR estimator, i.e.,
\hat{\theta}^{(3)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}=\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}
. Moreover, this estimator has minimum variance among all the G-MIPS estimators
\{\hat{\theta}^{(j)}_{\textup{G-MIPS}}\}_{0\leq j\leq k}
, as the representation
R^{(3)}
contains precisely the least amount of information necessary to obtain the outcome
Y
. In other words,
Y
itself serves as the ‘best embedding’ of covariate-action pair
R^{(0)}
which contains all relevant information regarding
Y
. We verify this empirically in Appendix
6.6.2
by reproducing the experimental setup in
Saito and Joachims 2022
along with the MR baseline. Additionally, the MR estimator does not rely on assumptions like
6.4.1
for unbiasedness.

In addition to this, solving the regression problem in Eq. (
6.2
) will typically be more difficult when
R
is higher dimensional (as is likely to be the case for many choices of embeddings
R
), leading to high bias. In contrast, for MR the embedding
R=Y
is one dimensional and therefore the regression problem is significantly easier to solve and yields lower bias. Our empirical results in Appendix
6.6
confirm this.

6.4.2
Doubly robust G-MIPS estimators

Consider the setup for the G-MIPS estimator shown in Figure
6.1
. In this case, we can derive a doubly robust extension of the G-MIPS estimator, denoted as GM-DR, which uses an estimate of the conditional mean
\tilde{\mu}(r)\approx\mathbb{E}[Y\mid R=r]
as a control variate to decrease the variance of G-MIPS estimator. This can be explicitly written as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{DM-DR}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r_{i})}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r_{i})}\,(y_{i}-\tilde{\mu}(r_{i}))+\tilde{\eta}(\pi^{*}).

(6.4)

where
\tilde{\eta}(\pi^{*})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\tilde{\mu}(r^{\prime})\,p_{\pi^{*}}(r^{\prime}\mid x_{i})
is the analogue of the direct method. Here,
\mathcal{R}
denotes the space of the possible of the representations
R
1
1

1

the
\sum_{r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}
can be replaced with
\int_{r^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}
when
\mathcal{R}
is continuous
. Moreover, given the density
p(r\mid x,a)
, we can compute
p_{\pi^{*}}(r\mid x)
using

p_{\pi^{*}}(r\mid x)=\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}p(r\mid x,a^{\prime})\,\pi^{*}(a^{\prime}\mid x).

It is straightforward to extend ideas from
Dudík et al. 2014b
to show that estimator
\tilde{\theta}_{\textup{DM-DR}}
is doubly robust in that it will yield accurate value estimates if either the importance weights
\frac{p_{\pi^{*}}(r)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(r)}
or the outcome model
\tilde{\mu}(r)
is well estimated.

There is no analogous DR extension of the MR estimator

A consequence of considering the embedding
R=Y
(as in MR) is that in this case we do not have an analogous doubly robust extension as above. To see why this is the case, note that when
R=Y
, we get that
\tilde{\mu}(r)=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid R=r]=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid Y=y]=y
. If we substitute this
\tilde{\mu}(r)
in (
6.4
), we are simply left with
\tilde{\eta}(\pi^{*})
on the right hand side (as the first term cancels out). This means that the resulting estimator does not retain the doubly robust nature as we no longer obtain an accurate estimate if either the outcome model or the importance ratios are well estimated.

6.5
Application to causal inference

In this section, we investigate the application of the MR estimator for the estimation of average treatment effect (ATE). In this setting, we suppose that
\mathcal{A}=\{0,1\}
, and the goal is to estimate ATE defined as follows:

\textup{ATE}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[Y(1)-Y(0)]

Here, we use the potential outcomes notation
(
Robins 1986
)
to denote the outcome under a deterministic policy
\pi^{*}(a^{\prime}\mid x)=\mathbbm{1}(a^{\prime}=a)
as
Y(a)
.

Specifically, the IPW estimator applied to ATE estimation yields:

\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{IPW}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(a_{i},x_{i})\times y_{i},

where

\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(a,x)\coloneqq\frac{\mathbbm{1}(a=1)-\mathbbm{1}(a=0)}{\pi^{b}(a|x)}.

Similarly, the MR estimator can be written as

\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{MR}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{\textup{ATE}}(y_{i})\times y_{i},

where

w_{\textup{ATE}}(y)=\frac{p_{\pi^{(1)}}(y)-p_{\pi^{(0)}}(y)}{p_{\pi^{b}}(y)},

and
\pi^{(a)}(a^{\prime}\mid x)\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}(a^{\prime}=a)
for
a\in\{0,1\}
.

Again, using the fact that
w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]
, we can obtain
w_{\textup{ATE}}
by minimising a simple mean-squared loss:

\displaystyle w_{\textup{ATE}}=\arg\min_{f}\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\Big[\frac{\mathbbm{1}(A=1)-\mathbbm{1}(A=0)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}-f(Y)\Big]^{2}.

Proposition 6.5.1

(Variance comparison with IPW ATE estimator)
.

When the weights
\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(a,x)
and
w_{\textup{ATE}}(y)
are known exactly, we have that
\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{MR}}]\leq\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{IPW}}]
. Specifically,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{MR}}]=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{Var}\left[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)|Y\right]\,Y^{2}\right]\geq 0.

Proof 6.5.2

(Proof of Proposition
6.5.1
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{MR}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\left(\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y]-\textup{Var}[w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\,Y]\right).

(6.5)

Using the tower law of variance, we get that

\displaystyle\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y\mid Y]]+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y\mid Y]]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y]+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}[w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\,Y]+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}].

Putting this together with (
6.5
) we obtain,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{IPW}}]-\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{MR}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}],

which straightforwardly leads to the result.

Given the above definitions, the IPW estimator for
\mathbb{E}[Y(a)]
would only consider datapoints with
A=a
, as it weights the samples using the policy ratios
\mathbbm{1}(A=a)/\pi^{b}(A|X)
which are only non-zero when
A=a
.
This is however not the case with the MR estimator, as it uses the weights
p_{\pi^{*}}(Y)/p_{\pi^{b}}(Y)
which are not necessarily zero for
A\neq a
. Therefore, MR uses all evaluation datapoints
\mathcal{D}
when estimating
\mathbb{E}[Y(a)]
. The MR estimator therefore leads to a more efficient use of evaluation data in this example.

Likewise, the doubly robust (DR) estimator applied to ATE estimation yields,

\displaystyle\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{DR}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(a_{i},x_{i})\,\left(y_{i}-\hat{\mu}(a_{i},x_{i})\right)+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\hat{\mu}(1,x_{i})-\hat{\mu}(0,x_{i})\right),

where
\hat{\mu}(a,x)\approx\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x,A=a]
.
Like in classical off-policy evaluation, DR yields an accurate estimator of ATE when either the weights
\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(a,x)
or the outcome model i.e.,
\hat{\mu}(a,x)=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X=x,A=a]
, are well estimated.
However, despite this doubly robust nature of the estimator, we can show that the variance of the DR estimator may be higher than that of the MR estimator in many cases. The following result formalises this variance comparison between the DR and MR estimators, and is analogous to the result in Proposition
2.3.4
derived for classical off-policy evaluation.

Proposition 6.5.3

(Variance comparison with DR ATE estimator)
.

When the weights
\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(a,x)
and
w_{\textup{ATE}}(y)
are known exactly,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{DR}}]-\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{MR}}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\geq\frac{1}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{Var}\left[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y\mid Y\right]-\textup{Var}\left[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mu(A,X)\mid X\right]\right],

where
\mu(A,X)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X,A]
.

Proof 6.5.4

(Proof of Proposition
6.5.3
)
.

Using the law of total variance, we get that

\displaystyle n\,\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{DR}}]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+(\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X))]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+(\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X))\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(Y-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+(\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X))\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle=\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+(\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X))]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}[\rho^{2}_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]].

Again, using the law of total variance we can rewrite the first term on the RHS above as,

\displaystyle\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+(\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X))]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+(\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X))\mid X]]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+(\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X))\mid X]]

\displaystyle\quad\geq\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+(\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X))\mid X]]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,(\mu(A,X)-\hat{\mu}(A,X))+\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]],

where, in the second last step above we use the fact that

\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\hat{\mu}(A,X)\mid X]=\hat{\mu}(1,X)-\hat{\mu}(0,X).

Putting this together, we get that

\displaystyle n\,\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{DR}}]\geq\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\mathbb{E}[\rho^{2}_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]].

Therefore,

\displaystyle n\,(\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{DR}}]-\textup{Var}[\widehat{\textup{ATE}}_{\textup{MR}}])

\displaystyle\quad\geq\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\mathbb{E}[\rho^{2}_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\textup{Var}[Y\mid X,A]]-\textup{Var}[w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\,Y]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y\mid X,A]]-\textup{Var}[w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\,Y]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y]-\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y\mid X,A]]

\displaystyle\qquad-\textup{Var}[w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\,Y]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y]+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y\mid X,A]]-\textup{Var}[w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\,Y]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]+\textup{Var}[w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\,Y]+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y\mid X,A]]-\textup{Var}[w_{\textup{ATE}}(Y)\,Y]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]-\textup{Var}[\mathbb{E}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,Y\mid X,A]]+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]

\displaystyle\quad=\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)]-\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)\mid X]]-\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\,\mu(A,X)]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}[\textup{Var}[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y]\,Y^{2}]

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\left[\textup{Var}\left[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y\right]\,Y^{2}-\textup{Var}\left[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mu(A,X)\mid X\right]\right].

Proposition
6.5.3
shows that if
\textup{Var}\left[Y\,\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y\right]
is greater than the conditional variance
\textup{Var}\left[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mu(A,X)\mid X\right]
on average, the variance of the MR estimator will be less than that of the DR estimator. Intuitively, this is likely to happen when the dimension of context space
\mathcal{X}
is high because in this case, the conditional variance over
X
and
A
,
\textup{Var}\left[Y\,\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mid Y\right]
is likely to be greater than the conditional variance over
A
,
\textup{Var}\left[\rho_{\textup{ATE}}(A,X)\mu(A,X)\mid X\right]
.

6.6
Experimental Results

In this section, we provide additional experimental details for the results presented in the main text. We also include extensive experimental results to provide further empirical evidence in favour of the MR estimator.

Computational details

We ran our experiments on Intel(R) Xeon(R) CPU E5-2690 v4 @ 2.60GHz with 8GB RAM
per core. We were able to use 150 CPUs in parallel to iterate over different configurations and seeds.
However, we would like to note that for each run our algorithms only requires 1 CPU and at most 30 minutes to run as our neural networks are relatively small. Throughout our experiments, whenever the outcome
Y
was continuous, we used a fully connected neural network with three hidden layers with 512, 256 and 32 nodes respectively (and ReLU activation function) to estimate the weights
\hat{w}(y)
. On the other hand, when the outcome is discrete we can directly estimate
\hat{w}(y)\approx\mathbb{E}[\hat{\rho}(A,X)\mid Y=y]
by calculating the sample mean of
\hat{\rho}(A,X)
on samples with
Y=y
. Additionally, for each configuration of parameters in our experiments, we ran experiments for 10 different seeds (in {0, 1, …, 9}).

6.6.1
Alternative methodology of estimating MR

In addition to the OPE baselines like IPW, DM and DR estimators considered in the main text, we also include empirically investigate an alternative methodology of estimating MR.
Below we describe this methodology, denoted as ‘MR (alt)’, in greater detail:

MR (alt)

Recall our definition of MR estimator:

\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w(y_{i})\,y_{i}.

In the main text, we propose estimating the weights
w(y)
first and using this to estimate
\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}
using the above expression. Alternatively, we can estimate
h(y)\coloneqq y\,w(y)
using

\displaystyle h=\arg\min_{f}\,\mathbb{E}_{\pi^{b}}\left[\Bigg(Y\,\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}-f(Y)\Bigg)^{2}\right].

Subsequently, the MR estimator can be written as:

\hat{\theta}_{\textup{MR}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(y_{i}).

We refer to this alternative methodology as ‘MR-alt’ and compare it empirically against the original methodology (which we simply refer to as ‘MR’).
In general, it is difficult to say which of the two methods will perform better. Intuitively speaking, in cases where the behaviour of the quantity
Y\,\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}
with varying
Y
is ‘smoother’ than that of
\frac{\pi^{*}(A|X)}{\pi^{b}(A|X)}
, the alternative method is expected to perform better. Our empirical results in the next sections show that the relative performance of the two methods varies for different data generating mechanisms.

6.6.2
Synthetic data experiments

Here, we include additional experimental details for the synthetic data experiments presented in Section
2.5.1
for completeness. For this experiment, we use the same setup as the synthetic data experiment in
Saito and Joachims 2022
, reproduced by reusing their code with minor modifications.

Setup

Here, we sample the
d
-dimensional context vectors
x
from a standard normal distribution.
The setup used also includes
3
-dimensional categorical action embeddings
E\in\mathcal{E}
, which are sampled from the following conditional distribution given action
A=a
,

p(e\mid a)=\prod_{k=1}^{3}\frac{\exp{(\alpha_{a,e_{k}})}}{\sum_{e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}}\exp{(\alpha_{a,e^{\prime}})}},

which is independent of the context
X
.
\{\alpha_{a,e_{k}}\}
is a set of parameters sampled independently from the standard normal distribution. Each dimension of
\mathcal{E}
has a cardinality of
10
, i.e.,
\mathcal{E}_{k}=\{1,2,\dots,10\}
.

Reward function

The expected reward is then defined as:

q(x,e)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\eta_{k}\cdot(x^{T}\,M\,x_{e_{k}}+\theta_{x}^{T}\,x+\theta_{e}^{T}\,x_{e_{k}}),

where
M
,
\theta_{x}
and
\theta_{e}
are parameter matrices or vectors sampled from a uniform distribution with range
[-1,1]
.
x_{e_{k}}
is a context vector corresponding to the
k
-th dimension of the action embedding, which is unobserved to the estimators.
\eta_{k}
specifies the importance of the
k
-th dimension of the action embedding, sampled from Dirichlet distribution so that
\sum_{k=1}^{3}\eta_{k}=1
.

Behaviour and target policies

The behaviour policy
\pi^{b}
is defined by applying the softmax function to
q(x,a)=\mathbb{E}[q(X,E)\mid A=a,X=x]
as

\pi^{b}(a\mid x)=\frac{\exp{(-q(x,a))}}{\sum_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}\exp{(-q(x,a^{\prime}))}}.

For the target policy, we define the class of parametric policies,

\pi^{\alpha^{\ast}}(a|x)=\alpha^{\ast}\,\mathbbm{1}(a=\arg\max_{a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}}q(x,a^{\prime}))+\frac{1-\alpha^{\ast}}{|\mathcal{A}|},

where
\alpha^{\ast}\in[0,1]
controls the shift between the behaviour and target policies. As shown in the main text, as
\alpha^{\ast}\rightarrow 1
, the shift between behaviour and target policies increases.

Baselines

In the main text, we compare MR with DM, IPW, DR and MIPS estimators. In addition to these baselines, here we also consider Switch-DR
(
Wang et al. 2017b
)
and DR with Optimistic Shrinkage (DRos)
(
Su et al. 2020
)
.
Following
Saito and Joachims 2022
, we use the random forest
(
Breiman 2001
)
along with 2-fold cross-fitting
(
Newey and Robins 2018
)
to obtain
\hat{q}(x,e)
for DR and DM methods.
To estimate
p_{\pi^{b}}(a\mid x,e)
for MIPS estimator, we use logistic regression.
We also include the results for MR estimated using the alternative methodology described in Section
6.6.1
. We refer to this as ‘MR (alt)’.

Estimation of behaviour policy
\widehat{\pi}^{b}
and marginal ratio
\hat{w}(y)

We do not assume that the true behaviour policy
\pi^{b}
is known, and therefore estimate
\widehat{\pi}^{b}
using the available training data.
For the MR estimator, we estimate the behaviour policy using a random forest classifier trained on 50% of the training data and use the rest of the training data to estimate the marginal ratios
\hat{w}(y)
using multi-layer perceptrons (MLP). Moreover, for a fair comparison we use a different behaviour policy estimate
\widehat{\pi}^{b}
for all other baselines which is trained on the entire training data.

,
n_{a}=100
,
\alpha^{\ast}=0.8

(b)

d=5000
,
n_{a}=250
,
\alpha^{\ast}=0.8

(c)

,
n_{a}=250
,
\alpha^{\ast}=1.0

for different choices of parameters.

Results

For this experiment, the results are computed over 10 different sets of logged data replicated with different seeds, and in Figures
6.3
-
6.6
we use a total of
m=5000
training data.

Varying size of evaluation data
n

Figure
6.3
shows that MR outperforms the other baselines, in terms of MSE and squared bias, when the number of evaluation data
n\leq 1000
. Additionally, we observe that in this experiment, MR estimated using our original methods (‘MR’), yields better results than the alternative method of estimating MR (‘MR (alt)’). Moreover, while the variance of DM is lower than that of MR, the DM method has a high bias and consequently a high MSE. We note that while the difference between MSE and variance of MIPS and MR estimators decreases with increasing evaluation data size, MR still outperforms MIPS in terms of both MSE and variance.

Varying
\alpha^{\ast}

Figure
6.4
shows the results with increasing policy shift. It can be seen that overall MR methods achieve the smallest MSE with increasing policy shift. Moreover, the difference between MSE and variance of MR and IPW/DR methods increases with increasing policy shift, showing that MR performs especially better than these baselines when the difference between behaviour and target policies is large. Similarly, we observe in Figure
6.4
that as the shift between the behaviour and target policy increases with increasing
\alpha^{\ast}
, so does the difference between the MSE and variance of MR and the MIPS estimators. This shows that generally MR outperforms MIPS estimator in terms of variance and MSE, and that MR performs especially better than MIPS as the difference between behaviour and target policies increases.

Varying
d
and
n_{a}

Figures
6.5
and
6.6
show that MR outperforms the other baselines as the context dimensions and/or number of actions increase. In fact, these figures show that MR is significantly robust to increasing dimensions of action and context spaces, whereas baselines like IPW and DR perform poorly in large action spaces.

Varying
m

Figure
6.8
shows the results with increasing number of training data
m
. We again observe that the MR methods ‘MR’ and ‘MR (alt)’ outperforms the other baselines in terms of the MSE and squared bias even when the number of training data is low. Moreover, the variance of both the MR estimators continues to improve with increasing number of training data.

In this experiment, we observe that overall ‘MR (alt)’ performs worse than the original MR estimator (‘MR’ in the figures). However, as we observe in Appendix
6.6.5
, this does not happen consistently across all experiments, which suggests that the comparative performance of the two MR methods depends on the data generating mechanism.
