7.5
How the miscoverage depends on
\hat{P}(y\mid x,a)

Proposition 7.5.1
.

Let

\displaystyle\tilde{w}(x,y)\coloneqq\frac{\int\hat{P}(y\mid x,a)\pi^{*}(a\mid x)\mathrm{d}a}{\int\hat{P}(y\mid x,a)\pi^{b}(a\mid x)\mathrm{d}a}.

Assume that

\hat{P}(y\mid x,a)/P(y\mid x,a)\in[1/\Gamma,\Gamma]
for some
\Gamma\geq 1
.
Then,

\Delta_{w}\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim P^{\pi^{b}}_{X,Y}}\mid\tilde{w}(X,Y)-w(X,Y)\mid\leq\Gamma^{2}-1.

Proof 7.5.2
.

In this proof, we investigate the error of the weights as a function of the error in
\hat{P}(y\mid x,a)
. Therefore, to isolate this effect we ignore the Monte Carlo error, and assume known behavioural policy
\pi^{b}
.

Under the assumption above, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1/\Gamma\int P(y\mid x,a)\pi^{*}(a\mid x)\mathrm{d}a}{\Gamma\int P(y\mid x,a)\pi^{b}(a\mid x)\mathrm{d}a}\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{w}(x,y)\leq\frac{\Gamma\int P(y\mid x,a)\pi^{*}(a\mid x)\mathrm{d}a}{1/\Gamma\int P(y\mid x,a)\pi^{b}(a\mid x)\mathrm{d}a}.

\displaystyle\implies\frac{1}{\Gamma^{2}}w(x,y)\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{w}(x,y)\leq\Gamma^{2}w(x,y)

This means that,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{\Gamma^{2}}-1\right)w(x,y)\leq

\displaystyle\tilde{w}(x,y)-w(x,y)\leq(\Gamma^{2}-1)w(x,y)

So,

\displaystyle\mid\tilde{w}(x,y)-w(x,y)\mid\leq(\Gamma^{2}-1)w(x,y)

And therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim P^{\pi^{b}}_{X,Y}}\mid\tilde{w}(X,Y)-w(X,Y)\mid\leq(\Gamma^{2}-1)\mathbb{E}_{(X,Y)\sim P^{\pi^{b}}_{X,Y}}[w(X,Y)]=\Gamma^{2}-1

Chapter 8
Causal Falsification of Digital Twins

\minitoc

8.1
Notation

z_{t:t^{\prime}}

The sequence of elements
(z_{t},\ldots,z_{t^{\prime}})
(or the empty sequence when
t>t^{\prime}
)

\mathcal{Z}_{t:t^{\prime}}
(where each
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
is a set)

The cartesian product
\mathcal{Z}_{t}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{Z}_{t^{\prime}}
(or the empty set when
t>t^{\prime}
)

Z_{t:t^{\prime}}(a_{1:t^{\prime}})

The sequence of potential outcomes
Z_{t}(a_{1:t}),\ldots,Z_{t^{\prime}}(a_{1:t^{\prime}})
(or the empty sequence when
t>t^{\prime}
)

\mathrm{Law}[Z]

The distribution of the random variable
Z

\mathrm{Law}[Z\mid M]

The conditional distribution of
Z
given
M
, where
M
is either an event or a random variable

Z\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}Z^{\prime}

The random variables
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are almost surely equal, i.e.
\mathbb{P}(Z=Z^{\prime})=1

Z\perp\!\!\!\perp Z^{\prime}

The random variables
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are independent

Z\perp\!\!\!\perp Z^{\prime}\mid Z^{\prime\prime}

The random variables
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are conditionally independent given the random variable
Z^{\prime\prime}

\mathbbm{1}(E)

Indicator function of some event
E

8.2
Proof of Proposition
4.2.2
(unconditional form of interventional correctness)

Proof 8.2.1
.

Fix any choice of
a_{1:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:T}
.
By disintegrating
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0},\widehat{X}_{1:T}(X_{0},a_{1:T})]
and
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0:T}(a_{1:T})]
along their common
\mathcal{X}_{0}
-marginal (which is namely
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0}]
), it holds that

\mathrm{Law}[X_{0},\widehat{X}_{1:T}(X_{0},a_{1:T})]=\mathrm{Law}[X_{0:T}(a_{1:T})]

(8.1)

if and only if

\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{1:T}(X_{0},a_{1:T})\mid X_{0}=x_{0}]=\mathrm{Law}[X_{1:T}(a_{1:T})\mid X_{0}=x_{0}]

(8.2)

for
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0}]
-almost all
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
But now, our definition of
\widehat{X}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})
in terms of
h_{t}
and
U_{1:t}
means we can write
\widehat{X}_{1:T}(X_{0},a_{1:T})=\boldsymbol{h}(X_{0},a_{1:T},U_{1:T})
,
where

\boldsymbol{h}(x_{0},a_{1:T},u_{1:T})\coloneqq(h_{1}(x_{0},a_{1},u_{1}),\ldots,h_{T}(x_{0},a_{1:T},u_{1:T})).

For all
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
and measurable
B_{1:T}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{1:T}
, we then have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})](B_{1:T})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}(\boldsymbol{h}(x_{0},a_{1:T},U_{1:T})\in B_{1:T})]

\displaystyle=\int\mathbbm{1}(\boldsymbol{h}(x_{0},a_{1:T},u_{1:T})\in B_{1:T})\,\mathrm{Law}[U_{1:T}](\mathrm{d}u_{1:T}).

It is standard to show that the right-hand side is a Markov kernel in
x_{0}
and
B_{1:T}
.
Moreover, for any measurable
B_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{B_{0}}\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})](B_{1:T})\,\mathrm{Law}[X_{0}](\mathrm{d}x_{0})

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{B_{0}}\left[\int\mathbbm{1}(\boldsymbol{h}(x_{0},a_{1:T},u_{1:T})\in B_{1:T})\,\mathrm{Law}[U_{1:T}](\mathrm{d}u_{1:T})\right]\,\mathrm{Law}[X_{0}](\mathrm{d}x_{0})

\displaystyle\qquad=\int\mathbbm{1}(x_{0}\in B_{0},\boldsymbol{h}(x_{0},a_{1:T},u_{1:T})\in B_{1:T})\,\mathrm{Law}[X_{0},U_{1:T}](\mathrm{d}x_{0},\mathrm{d}u_{1:T})

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathrm{Law}[X_{0},\widehat{X}_{1:T}(X_{0},a_{1:T})](B_{0:T}),

where the second step follows because
X_{0}\perp\!\!\!\perp U_{1:T}
.
It therefore follows that
(x_{0},B_{1:T})\mapsto\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})](B_{1:T})
is a regular conditional distribution of
\widehat{X}_{1:T}(X_{0},a_{1:T})
given
X_{0}
, i.e.

\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})]=\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{1:T}(X_{0},a_{1:T})\mid X_{0}=x_{0}]\qquad\text{for $\mathrm{Law}[X_{0}]$-almost all $x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}$.}

Substituting this into (
8.2
), we see that (
8.1
) holds if and only if

\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})]=\mathrm{Law}[X_{1:T}(a_{1:T})\mid X_{0}=x_{0}]

for
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0}]
-almost all
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
The result now follows since
a_{1:T}
was arbitrary.

8.3
Online prediction

8.3.1
Correctness in the online setting

A distinguishing feature of many digital twins is their ability to integrate real-time information obtained from sensors in their environment
(
Barricelli et al. 2019
)
.
It is therefore relevant to consider a setting in which a twin is used repeatedly to make a sequence of predictions over time, each time taking all previous information into account.
One way to formalize this is to instantiate our model for the twin at each timestep.
For example, we could represent the predictions made by the twin at
t=0
after observing initial covariates
x_{0}
as potential outcomes
(\widehat{X}^{1}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T}):a_{1:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:T})
, similar to what we did in the main text.
We could then represent the predictions made by the twin after some action
a_{1}
is taken and an additional observation
x_{1}
is made via potential outcomes
(\widehat{X}^{2}_{2:T}(x_{0:1},a_{1:T}):a_{2:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{2:T})
.
More generally, for
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
, we could introduce potential outcomes
(\widehat{X}^{t}_{t:T}(x_{0:t-1},a_{1:T}):a_{t:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{t:T})
to represent the predictions that the twin would make at time
t
after the observations
x_{0:t-1}
are made and the actions
a_{1:t-1}
are taken.

This extended model requires a new definition of correctness than our Definition
4.2.1
from the main text.
A natural approach is to say that the twin is correct in this new setting if

\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{t:T}^{t}(x_{0:t-1},a_{1:T})]=\mathrm{Law}[X_{t:T}(a_{1:T})\mid X_{0:t-1}(a_{1:t-1})=x_{0:t-1}]

(8.3)

for all
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
,
a_{1:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:T}
, and
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0:t-1}(a_{1:t-1})]
-almost all
x_{0:t-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{0:t-1}
.
A twin with this property would at each step be able to accurately simulate the future in light of previous information, use this to choose a next action to take, observe the result of doing so, and then repeat.
It is possible to show that (
8.3
) holds if and only if we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}^{1}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Law}[X_{1:T}(a_{1:T})\mid X_{0}=x_{0}]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}^{t}_{t:T}(x_{0:t-1},a_{1:T})]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}^{1}_{t:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})\mid\widehat{X}_{1:t-1}^{1}(x_{0},a_{1:t-1})=x_{1:t-1}]

for all
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
,
a_{1:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:T}
,
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0}]
-almost all
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and
\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{1:t-1}^{1}(x_{0},a_{1:t-1})]
-almost all
x_{1:t-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1:t-1}
.
The first condition here says that
\widehat{X}^{1}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})
must be interventionally correct in the sense of Definition
4.2.1
from the main text.
The second condition says that the predictions made by the twin across different timesteps must be internally consistent with each other insofar as their conditional distributions must align.
This holds automatically in many circumstances, such as if the predictions of the twin are obtained from a Bayesian model (for example), and otherwise could be checked numerically given the ability to run simulations from the twin, without the need to obtain data or refer to the real-world process in any way.
As such, the problem of assessing the correctness of the twin in this new sense primarily reduces to the problem of assessing the correctness of
\widehat{X}^{1}_{1:T}(x_{0},a_{1:T})
in the sense of Definition
4.2.1
in the main text, which motivates our focus on that condition.

8.3.2
Alternative notions of online correctness

An important and interesting subtlety arises in this context that is worth noting.
In general it does not follow that a twin correct in the sense of (
8.3
) satisfies

\mathrm{Law}[\widehat{X}_{t:T}^{t}(x_{0:t-1},a_{1:T})]\ =\mathrm{Law}[X_{t:T}(a_{1:T})\mid X_{0:t-1}(a_{1:t-1})=x_{0:t-1},A_{1:t-1}=a_{1:t-1}]

(8.4)

for all
a_{1:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:T}
, and
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0:t-1}(a_{1:t-1})\mid A_{1:t-1}=a_{1:t-1}]
-almost all
x_{0:t-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{0:t-1}
,
since in general it does not hold that

\mathrm{Law}[X_{t:T}(a_{1:T})\mid X_{0:t-1}(a_{1:t-1})=x_{0:t-1}]=\mathrm{Law}[X_{t:T}(a_{1:T})\mid X_{0:t-1}(a_{1:t-1})=x_{0:t-1},A_{1:t-1}=a_{1:t-1}].

for all
a_{1:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:T}
and
\mathrm{Law}[X_{0:t-1}(a_{1:t-1})\mid A_{1:t-1}=a_{1:t-1}]
-almost all
x_{0:t-1}\in\mathcal{X}_{0:t-1}
unless the actions
A_{1:t-1}
are unconfounded.
(Here as usual
A_{1:T}
denotes the actions of a behavioural agent; see Section
4.3
of the main text.)
In other words, a twin that is correct in the sense of (
8.3
) will make accurate predictions at time
t
when every action taken before time
t
was unconfounded (as occurs for example when the twin is directly in control of the decision-making process), but in general not when certain taken actions before time
t
were chosen by a behavioural agent with access to more context than is available to the twin (as may occur for example when the twin is used as a decision-support tool).
However, should it be desirable, our framework could be extended to encompass the alternative condition in (
8.4
) by relabelling the observed history
(X_{0:t-1}(A_{1:t-1}),A_{1:t-1})
as
X_{0}
, and then assessing the correctness of the potential outcomes
\widehat{X}_{t:T}^{t}(x_{0:t-1},a_{1:T})
in the sense of Definition
4.2.1
from the main text.

Overall, the “right” notion of correctness in this online setting is to some extent a design choice.
We believe our causal approach to twin assessment provides a useful framework for formulating and reasoning about these possibilities, and consider the investigation of assessment strategies for additional usage regimes to be an interesting direction for future work.

8.4
Proof of Theorem
4.3.1
(interventional distributions are not identifiable)

It is well-known in the causal inference literature that the interventional behaviour of the real-world process cannot be uniquely identified from observational data.
For completeness, we now provide a self-contained proof of this result in our notation.
Our statement here is lengthier than Theorem
4.3.1
in the main text in order to clarify what is meant by “uniquely identified”: intuitively, the idea is that there always exist distinct families of potential outcomes whose interventional behaviours differ and yet give rise to the same observational data.

Theorem 8.4.1
.

Suppose we have
a_{1:T}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:T}
such that
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:T}\neq a_{1:T})>0
.
Then there exist potential outcomes
(\tilde{X}_{0:T}(a_{1:T}^{\prime}):a_{1:T}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:T})
such that

(\tilde{X}_{0:T}(A_{1:T}),A_{1:T})\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}(X_{0:T}(A_{1:T}),A_{1:T}).

(8.5)

but for which
\mathrm{Law}[\tilde{X}_{0:T}(a_{1:t})]\neq\mathrm{Law}[X_{0:T}(a_{1:t})]
.

Proof 8.4.2
.

Our assumption that
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:T}\neq a_{1:T})>0
means there must exist some
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
such that
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t})>0
.
Since
\mathcal{X}_{t}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{t}}
, we may also choose some
x_{t}\in\mathcal{X}_{t}
with
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}(a_{1:t})=x_{t}\mid A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t})\neq 1
.
Then, for each
s\in\{0,\ldots,T\}
and
a_{1:s}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:s}
, define

\tilde{X}_{s}(a_{1:s}^{\prime})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})\,X_{t}(a_{1:t})+\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t})\,x_{t}&\text{if $s=t$ and $a_{1:s}^{\prime}=a_{1:t}$}\\
X_{s}(a_{1:s}^{\prime})&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

It is then easily checked that (
8.5
) holds, but

\displaystyle\mathrm{Law}[\tilde{X}_{t}(a_{1:t})]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Law}[\tilde{X}_{t}(a_{1:t})\mid A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})+\mathrm{Law}[\tilde{X}_{t}(a_{1:t})\mid A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Law}[X_{t}(a_{1:t})\mid A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})+\mathrm{Dirac}(x_{t})\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t})

\displaystyle\neq\mathrm{Law}[X_{t}(a_{1:t})\mid A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})+\mathrm{Law}[X_{t}(a_{1:t})\mid A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Law}[X_{t}(a_{1:t})],

from which the result follows.

8.5
Deterministic potential outcomes are unconfounded

In this section we expand on our earlier claim that, if the real-world process is deterministic, then the observational data is unconfounded.
We first make this claim precise.
By “deterministic”, we mean that there exist measurable functions
g_{t}
for
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
such that

X_{t}(a_{1:t})\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}g_{t}(X_{0:t-1}(a_{1:t-1}),a_{1:t})\qquad\text{for all $t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}$ and $a_{1:t}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:t}$.}

(8.6)

By “unconfounded”, we mean that the
sequential randomisation assumption (SRA)
introduced by Robins
(
Robins 1986
)
holds, i.e.

(X_{s}(a_{1:s}):s\in\{1,\ldots,T\},a_{1:s}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:s})\perp\!\!\!\perp A_{t}\mid X_{0:t-1}(A_{1:t-1}),A_{1:t-1}\qquad\text{for all $t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}$},

(8.7)

where
\perp\!\!\!\perp
denotes conditional independence.
Intuitively, this says that, apart from the historical observations
(X_{0:t-1}(A_{1:t-1}),A_{1:t-1})
, any additional factors that influence the agent’s choice of action
A_{t}
are independent of the behaviour of the real-world process.
The SRA provides a standard formulation of the notion of unconfoundedness in longitudinal settings such as ours (see
(
Tsiatis et al. 2019
, Chapter 5)
for a review).

It is now a standard exercise to show that (
8.6
) implies (
8.7
).
We include a proof below for completeness.
Key to this is the following straightforward Lemma.

Lemma 8.5.1
.

Suppose
U
and
V
are random variables such that, for some measurable function
g
, it holds that
U\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}g(V)
.
Then, for any other random variable
W
, we have

U\perp\!\!\!\perp W\mid V.

Proof 8.5.2
.

By standard properties of conditional expectations, for any measurable sets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, we have almost surely

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(U\in S_{1},W\in S_{2}\mid V)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}(g(V)\in S_{1})\,\mathbbm{1}(W\in S_{2})\mid V]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}(g(V)\in S_{1})\,\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}(W\in S_{2})\mid V]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}(U\in S_{1})\mid V]\,\mathbb{P}(W\in S_{2}\mid V)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(U\in S_{1}\mid V)\,\mathbb{P}(W\in S_{2}\mid V),

which gives the result.

It is now easy to see that (
8.6
) implies (
8.7
).
Indeed, by recursive substitution, it is straightforward to show that there exist measurable functions
\tilde{g}_{t}
for
t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}
such that

X_{t}(a_{1:t})\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}\tilde{g}_{t}(X_{0},a_{1:t})\qquad\text{for all $t\in\{1,\ldots,T\}$ and $a_{1:t}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:t}$},

and so

(X_{s}(a_{1:s}):s\in\{1,\ldots,T\},a_{1:s}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:s})=(\tilde{g}_{t}(X_{0},a_{1:s}):s\in\{1,\ldots,T\},a_{1:s}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:s}).

The right-hand side is now seen to be a measurable function of
X_{0}
and hence certainly of
(X_{0:t-1}(A_{1:t-1}),A_{1:t-1})
, so that the result follows by Lemma
8.5.1
.

8.6
Motivating toy example

We provide here a toy scenario that illustrates intuitively the pitfalls that may arise when assessing twins using observational data without properly accounting for causal considerations (including unmeasured confounding in particular).
Suppose a digital twin has been designed for a particular make of car, e.g. to facilitate autonomous driving
(
Allamaa et al. 2022
)
.
The twin simulates how quantities such as the velocity and fuel consumption of the car respond as certain inputs are applied to it, such as braking, acceleration, steering, etc.
We wish to assess the accuracy of this twin using a dataset obtained from a fleet of the same make.
The braking performance of these vehicles is significantly affected by the age of their brake pads: if these are fairly new, then an aggressive braking strategy will stop the car, while if these are old, then the same aggressive strategy will send the car into a skid that will reduce braking efficacy.
Brake pad age is not recorded in the data we have obtained, but
was
known to the drivers who operated these vehicles (e.g. perhaps they were aware of how recently their car was serviced), and so the drivers of cars with old brake pads tended to avoid braking aggressively out of safety concerns.

A naive approach to twin assessment in this situation would directly compare the outputs of the twin with the data and conclude the twin is accurate if these match closely.
However, in this scenario, the data contains a spurious relationship between braking strategy and the performance of the car: since aggressive braking is only observed for cars with new brake pads, the data appears to show that aggressive braking is effective at stopping the car, while in fact this is not the case for cars with older brake pads.
As such, the naive assessment approach would yield misleading information about the twin: a twin that captures only the behaviour of cars with newer brake pads would appear to be correct, while a twin that captures the full range of possibilities (i.e. regardless of brake pad age) would deviate from the observational data and appear therefore less accurate.
Figure
8.1
illustrates this pictorially under a toy model for this scenario.

Figure 8.1
:
The discrepancy between observational data and interventional behavior. The data only show the effect of aggressive braking on cars with new brake pads (blue). This differs from what
would
be observed if aggressive braking were applied to the entire fleet of cars, encompassing both those with old and new brake pads (red).

In the causal inference literature, any unmeasured quantity (e.g. brake pad age) that affects both some choice of action taken in the data (e.g. aggressive braking) and the resulting observation (e.g. speed) is referred to as an
unmeasured confounder
.
In general, whenever an unmeasured confounder is present, a potential discrepancy arises between how the real-world process was observed to behave in the dataset and how it
would
behave under certain interventions.
An obvious approach towards mitigating this possibility is to measure additional quantities that may affect the outcome of interest.
For example, if brake pad age were included in the data in the scenario above, then it would be possible to adjust for its effect on braking performance.
However, in many cases, gathering additional data may be costly or impractical.
Moreover, even if this strategy is pursued, it is rarely possible to rule out the possibility of unmeasured confounding altogether, especially for complicated real-world problems
(
Tsiatis et al. 2019
)
.
For example, in the scenario above, it is very conceivable that some other factor such as weather conditions could play a similar confounding role as brake pad age, and so would need also to be included in the data, and so on.
Analogous scenarios are also easily forthcoming for other application domains such as medicine and economics
(
Manski 1995
;
Tsiatis et al. 2019
;
Hernán and Robins 2020
)
.
As such, rather than attempting to sidestep the issue of unmeasured confounding, we instead propose a methodology for assessing twins using data that is robust to its presence.

8.7
Causal bounds

8.7.1
Proof of Theorem
4.4.1

Proof 8.7.1
.

We prove the lower bound; the upper bound is analogous.
It is easily checked that

\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}]\\
=\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})\\
+\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t},A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}).

(8.8)

If
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})>0
, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y(A_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(A_{1:t})\in B_{0:t},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y(A_{1:N})\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}],

where the second step follows because
\mathbb{P}(N=t\mid A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})=1
.
Similarly, if
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})>0
, then (
4.5
) implies

\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t},A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}]\geq y_{\mathrm{lo}}.

Substituting these results into (
8.8
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}]\\
\geq\mathbb{E}[Y(A_{1:t})\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})\\
+y_{\mathrm{lo}}\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}).

(8.9)

Now observe that the right-hand side of (
8.9
) is a convex combination with mixture weights
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})
and
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})
.
We can bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}(X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})}{\mathbb{P}(X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\mathbb{P}(X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})}{\mathbb{P}(X_{0:N}(a_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}(X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})}{\mathbb{P}(X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}),

(8.10)

where the inequality holds because
t\geq N
almost surely, and the second equality holds because the definition of
N
means

X_{0:N}(a_{1:N})\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}X_{0:N}(A_{1:N}).

As such, we can bound the convex combination in (
8.9
) from below by replacing its mixture weights with
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})
and
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(a_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})
, which shifts weight from the
\mathbb{E}[Y(A_{1:t})\mid A_{1:t}=a_{1:t},X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}]
term onto the
y_{\mathrm{lo}}
term.
This yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\geq\mathbb{E}[Y(A_{1:t})\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(a_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+y_{\mathrm{lo}}\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(a_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\mathbb{E}[Y(A_{1:t})\,\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})+y_{\mathrm{lo}}\,\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}].

8.7.2
Proof of Proposition
4.4.2

Proof 8.7.2
.

From the definition of
Y_{\mathrm{up}}
, we have straightforwardly

Q_{\mathrm{up}}=\mathbb{E}[Y(A_{1:t})\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N},A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})\\
+y_{\mathrm{up}}\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}).

A similar expression holds for
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
.
Subtracting these two expressions yields

Q_{\mathrm{up}}-Q_{\mathrm{lo}}=(y_{\mathrm{up}}-y_{\mathrm{lo}})\,(1-\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})).

Similar manipulations show that

\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{up}}]-\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}]=(y_{\mathrm{up}}-y_{\mathrm{lo}})\,(1-\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})),

and the result now follows.

8.7.3
Proof of Proposition
4.4.3
and discussion

Proof 8.7.3
.

We consider the case of the lower bound; the case of the upper bound is analogous.
Choose
x_{1:T}\in B_{1:T}
arbitrarily.
(Certainly some choice is always possible, since each
B_{s}
has positive measure and is therefore nonempty.)
Define

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{0}

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{s}(a_{1:s}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:s}=a_{1:s}^{\prime})\,X_{s}(a_{1:s}^{\prime})+\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:s}\neq a_{1:s}^{\prime})\,x_{s}\qquad\text{for each $s\in\{0,\ldots,T\}$ and $a_{1:s}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:s}$},

and similarly let

\tilde{Y}(a_{1:t}^{\prime})=\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}^{\prime})\,Y(a_{1:t}^{\prime})+\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}^{\prime})\,y_{\mathrm{lo}}\qquad\text{for all $a_{1:t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:t}$}.

It is easy to check that
(\tilde{X}_{0:T}(A_{1:T}),\tilde{Y}(A_{1:t}),A_{1:T})\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}(X_{0:T}(A_{1:T}),Y(A_{1:t}),A_{1:T})
.
But now we have directly
\tilde{Y}(a_{1:t})=Y_{\mathrm{lo}}
.
Moreover, it is easily checked from the definition of
N
and
\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})
that

\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}(X_{0:N}(A_{1:N}),x_{N+1:t}),

so that

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}(\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}\mathbbm{1}(\tilde{X}_{0:N}(a_{1:N})\in B_{0:N},x_{N+1:t}\in B_{N+1:t})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}\mathbbm{1}(X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})

since each
x_{s}\in B_{s}
.
Consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Y}(a_{1:t})\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}],

which gives the result.

8.7.4
Bounds on the conditional expectation given specific covariate values

Theorem
4.4.1
provides a bound on
\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}]
, i.e. the conditional expectation given the
event

\{X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}\}
, which is assumed to have positive probability.
We consider here the prospect of obtaining bounds on
\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})]
, i.e. the conditional expectation given the
value
of
X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})
.
For falsification purposes, this would provide a means for determining that twin is incorrect when it outputs specific values of
\widehat{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})
, rather than just that it is incorrect on average across all runs that output values
\widehat{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}
.

When
X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})
is discrete, Theorem
4.4.1
yields measurable functions
g_{\mathrm{lo}},g_{\mathrm{up}}:\mathcal{X}_{0:t}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

g_{\mathrm{lo}}(X_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))\leq\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})]\leq g_{\mathrm{up}}(X_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))\qquad\text{almost surely}.

(8.11)

In particular,
g_{\mathrm{lo}}(x_{0:t})
is obtained as the value of
\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}]
for
B_{0:t}\coloneqq\{x_{0:t}\}
, and similarly for
g_{\mathrm{up}}(x_{0:t})
.
Moreover, since the constants
y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}\in\mathbb{R}
in Theorem
4.4.1
were allowed to depend on
B_{0:t}
, and hence here on each choice of
x_{0:t}\in\mathcal{X}_{0:t}
, we may think of these now as measurable functions
y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}:\mathcal{X}_{0:t}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

y_{\mathrm{lo}}(X_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))\leq Y(a_{1:t})\leq y_{\mathrm{up}}(X_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))\qquad\text{almost surely}.

(8.12)

In other words, when
X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})
is discrete, Theorem
4.4.1
provides bounds on the conditional expectation of
Y(a_{1:t})
given the value of
X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})
whenever we have
y_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
y_{\mathrm{up}}
such that (
8.12
) holds.

When
\mathbb{P}(X_{1:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{1:t})>0
, a fairly straightforward modification of the proof of Theorem
4.4.1
yields bounds of the following form:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}\mid X_{0},X_{1:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{1:N}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0},X_{1:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{1:t}]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{up}}\mid X_{0},X_{1:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{1:N}]\qquad\text{almost surely}.

(8.13)

In particular, this holds regardless of whether or not
X_{0}
is discrete.
In turn, if
X_{1:t}(a_{1:t})
is discrete, then by a similar argument as was given in the previous subsection, this yields almost sure bounds on
\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0:t}(a_{1:t})]
of the form in (
8.11
), provided (
8.12
) holds.
Alternatively, by taking
B_{1:t}\coloneqq\mathcal{X}_{1:t}
, (
8.13
) yields bounds of the form

\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}\mid X_{0}]\leq\mathbb{E}[Y(a_{1:t})\mid X_{0}]\leq\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{up}}\mid X_{0}].

If the action sequence
a_{1:t}
is thought of as a single choice of an action from the extended action space
\mathcal{A}_{1:t}
, then this recovers the bounds originally proposed by
Manski 1990
, which allowed conditioning on potentially continuous pre-treatment covariates corresponding to our
X_{0}
.

8.7.5
Proof of Theorem
4.4.4
and discussion

Proof 8.7.4
.

Suppose we have a permissible
g_{\mathrm{lo}}
.
(The case of
g_{\mathrm{up}}
is analogous).
Choose
x_{1:T}\in\mathcal{X}_{1:T}
arbitrarily, and define new potential outcomes

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{0}

\displaystyle\coloneqq X_{0}

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{r}(a_{1:r}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:r}=a_{1:r}^{\prime})\,X_{r}(a_{1:r}^{\prime})+\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:r}\neq a_{1:r}^{\prime})\,x_{r}\qquad\text{for $r\in\{1,\ldots,T\}$ and $a_{1:r}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:r}$}.

Similarly, define

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}(a_{1:t}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}^{\prime})\,Y(a_{1:t}^{\prime})+\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}^{\prime})\,y_{\mathrm{lo}}(\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}^{\prime}))\qquad\text{for all $a_{1:t}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:t}$}.

It immediately follows that

(\tilde{X}_{0:T}(A_{1:T}),\tilde{Y}(A_{1:t}),A_{1:T})\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}(X_{0:T}(A_{1:T}),Y(A_{1:t}),A_{1:T}).

Moreover, it is easily checked that

y_{\mathrm{lo}}(\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))\leq\tilde{Y}(a_{1:t})\leq y_{\mathrm{up}}(\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))\qquad\text{almost surely}.

As such, since
g_{\mathrm{lo}}
is permissible, we must have, almost surely,

\displaystyle g_{\mathrm{lo}}(\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Y}(a_{1:t})\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})]

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Y}(A_{1:t})\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}),A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}]\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))\\
+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Y}(a_{1:t})\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}),A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}]}_{=y_{\mathrm{lo}}(\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))}\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t}\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})).\end{multlined}

Now, by our definition of
\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})
, we have almost surely

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}(A_{1}\neq a_{1})\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}(A_{1}\neq a_{1},\tilde{X}_{s}(a_{1:s})=x_{s})\,\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}(A_{1}\neq a_{1})\,\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t},\tilde{X}_{s}(a_{1:s})=x_{s})\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})]

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}(A_{1}\neq a_{1})\,\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t},X_{s}(A_{1:s})=x_{s})\mid\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})]

\displaystyle=0,

where the last step follows by our assumption that
\mathbb{P}(X_{s}(A_{1:s})=x_{s})=0
.
Combining this with (
8.7.4
), we get, almost surely,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}(A_{1}\neq a_{1})\,g_{\mathrm{lo}}(X_{0},x_{1:t})

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}(A_{1}\neq a_{1})\,g_{\mathrm{lo}}(\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))

\displaystyle\leq\mathbbm{1}(A_{1}\neq a_{1})\,y_{\mathrm{lo}}(\tilde{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}(A_{1}\neq a_{1})\,y_{\mathrm{lo}}(X_{0},x_{1:t}).

(8.16)

Now let
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{0}
be the value such that
\mathbb{P}(X_{0}=x_{0})=1
.
Using our assumption that
\mathbb{P}(A_{1}\neq a_{1})>0
and the fact that
x_{1:t}
was arbitrary, we obtain

g_{\mathrm{lo}}(x_{0:t})\leq y_{\mathrm{lo}}(x_{0:t})\qquad\text{for all $x_{1:t}\in\mathcal{X}_{1:t}$}.

The result now follows.

To gain intuition for the phenomenon underlying Theorem
4.4.4
, consider a simplified model consisting of
\mathcal{X}
-valued potential outcomes
(X(a^{\prime}):a\in\mathcal{A})
,
\mathbb{R}
-valued potential outcomes
(Y(a^{\prime}):a\in\mathcal{A})
, and an
\mathcal{A}
-valued random variable
A
representing the choice of action.
(This constitutes a special case of our setup with
T=1
and
\mathcal{X}_{0}
taken to be a singleton set.)
Suppose moreover that the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X(A)=x)

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{for all $x\in\mathcal{X}$}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(A=a)

\displaystyle<1.

We then have

\mathbb{E}[Y(a)\mid X(a)]\overset{\mathrm{a.s.}}{=}\mathbb{E}[Y(A)\mid X(A),A=a]\,\mathbb{P}(A=a\mid X(a))+\mathbb{E}[Y(a)\mid X(a),A\neq a]\,\mathbb{P}(A\neq a\mid X(a)).

(8.17)

But now, since the behaviour of
X(a)
is only observed on
\{A=a\}
, for any given value of
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we cannot rule out the possibility that

X(a)=\mathbbm{1}(A=a)\,X(A)+\mathbbm{1}(A\neq a)\,x\qquad\text{almost surely}.

In turn, since
\mathbb{P}(A=a)>0
, this would imply
\mathbb{P}(X(a)=x)>0
, and, since
\mathbb{P}(X(A)=x)=0
, that
\mathbb{P}(A=a\mid X(a)=x)=0
.
From (
8.17
), this would yield

\mathbb{E}[Y(a)\mid X(a)=x]=\mathbb{E}[Y(a)\mid X(a)=x,A\neq a].

But now, since the behaviour of
Y(a)
is unobserved on
\{A\neq a\}
, intuitively speaking, the observational distribution does not provide any information about the value of the right-hand side, and therefore about the behaviour of
\mathbb{E}[Y(a)\mid X(a)]
more generally since
x\in\mathcal{X}
was arbitrary.

8.8
Hypothesis testing methodology

8.8.1
Validity of testing procedure

We show here that our procedure for testing
\widehat{Q}\geq Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
based on the one-sided confidence intervals
R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}
and
\widehat{R}^{\alpha}
has the correct probability of type I error, provided
R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}
and
\widehat{R}^{\alpha}
have the correct coverage probabilities.
In particular, the result below (which applies a standard union bound argument) shows that if
\widehat{Q}\geq Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
, then our test rejects (i.e.
\widehat{R}^{\alpha}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}
) with probability at most
\alpha
.
An analogous result is easily proven for testing
\widehat{Q}\leq Q_{\mathrm{up}}
also, with
R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}
replaced by a one-sided upper
(1-\alpha/2)
-confidence interval for
Q_{\mathrm{up}}
, and
\widehat{R}^{\alpha}
replaced by a one-sided lower
(1-\alpha/2)
-confidence interval for
\widehat{Q}
.

Proposition 8.8.1
.

Suppose that for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
we have random variables
\widehat{R}^{\alpha}
and
R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Q_{\mathrm{lo}}\geq R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}

(8.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{Q}\leq\widehat{R}^{\alpha})

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

(8.19)

If
\widehat{Q}\geq Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
, then
\mathbb{P}(\widehat{R}^{\alpha}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha})\leq\alpha
.

Proof 8.8.2
.

If
\widehat{Q}\geq Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
, then we have

\{\widehat{R}^{\alpha}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\}\subseteq\{\widehat{Q}>\widehat{R}^{\alpha}\}\cup\{Q_{\mathrm{lo}}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\}.

To see this, note that

(\{\widehat{Q}>\widehat{R}^{\alpha}\}\cup\{Q_{\mathrm{lo}}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\})^{c}=\{\widehat{Q}>\widehat{R}^{\alpha}\}^{c}\cap\{Q_{\mathrm{lo}}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\}^{c}=\{\widehat{Q}\leq\widehat{R}^{\alpha}\}\cap\{Q_{\mathrm{lo}}\geq R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\}\subseteq\{R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\leq\widehat{R}^{\alpha}\}.

As such,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\widehat{R}^{\alpha}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha})\leq\mathbb{P}(\{\widehat{Q}>\widehat{R}^{\alpha}\}\cup\{Q_{\mathrm{lo}}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\})\leq\mathbb{P}(\widehat{Q}>\widehat{R}^{\alpha})+\mathbb{P}(Q_{\mathrm{lo}}<R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha})\leq\alpha/2+\alpha/2=\alpha.

8.8.2
Unbiased sample mean estimates of
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
,
\widehat{Q}
, and
Q_{\mathrm{up}}

We use our data to obtain one-sided confidence intervals
R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}
and
\widehat{R}^{\alpha}
satisfying (
8.18
) and (
8.19
) as required by our procedure for testing
\widehat{Q}\geq Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
.
We use an analogous procedure to obtain confidence intervals for testing
\widehat{Q}\leq Q_{\mathrm{up}}
.
We tried two techniques for this: an exact method based on Hoeffding’s inequality, and an approximate method based on bootstrapping.
Conceptually, both are based on obtaining unbiased sample mean estimates of
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
\widehat{Q}
, which we describe now, before giving the particulars of each method in the next two subsections.

We begin with our sample mean estimator of
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
.
Recall that we assume access to a dataset
\mathcal{D}
consisting of i.i.d. copies of observational trajectories of the form

X_{0},A_{1},X_{1}(A_{1}),\ldots,A_{T},X_{T}(A_{1:T}).

Let
\mathcal{D}(a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
be the subset of trajectories in
\mathcal{D}
for which
X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}
.
Obtaining
\mathcal{D}(a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
is possible since the only random quantity that
N=\max\{0\leq s\leq t\mid A_{1:s}=a_{1:s}\}
depends on is
A_{1:t}
, which is included in the data.
We denote the cardinality of
\mathcal{D}(a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
by
n\coloneqq|\mathcal{D}(a_{1:t},B_{0:t})|
.
We then denote by
Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i)}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
the corresponding values of

\displaystyle Y_{\mathrm{lo}}

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t})\,f(X_{0:t}(A_{1:t}))+\mathbbm{1}(A_{1:t}\neq a_{1:t})\,y_{\mathrm{lo}}

obtained from each trajectory in
\mathcal{D}(a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
.
This is again possible since both terms only depends on the observational quantities
(X_{0:t}(A_{1:t}),A_{1:t})
.
It is easily seen that the values of
Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i)}
are i.i.d. and satisfy
\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i)}]=Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
.
As a result, the sample mean

{\mu_{\mathrm{lo}}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i)}

(8.20)

is an unbiased estimator of
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
.

We obtain an unbiased sample mean estimate of
\widehat{Q}
in a similar fashion as for
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
.
Recall that we assume access to a dataset
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t})
consisting of i.i.d. copies of

X_{0},\widehat{X}_{1}(X_{0},a_{1}),\ldots,\widehat{X}_{t}(X_{0},a_{1:t}).

Let
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
denote the subset of twin trajectories in
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t})
for which
(X_{0},\widehat{X}_{t}(X_{0},a_{1:t}))\in B_{0:t}
, and denote its cardinality by
\widehat{n}\coloneqq|\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t},B_{0:t})|
.
Then denote by
\widehat{Y}^{(i)}
for
i\in\{1\ldots,\widehat{n}\}
the corresponding values of

\widehat{Y}=f(X_{0},\widehat{X}_{1:t}(X_{0},a_{1:t}))

obtained from each trajectory in
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
.
It is easily seen that the values
\widehat{Y}^{(i)}
are i.i.d. (since the entries of
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t})
are) and satisfy
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{Y}^{(i)}]=\widehat{Q}
.
As a result, the sample mean

\widehat{\mu}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\widehat{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat{n}}\widehat{Y}^{(i)}

is an unbiased estimator of
\widehat{Q}
.

8.8.3
Exact confidence intervals via Hoeffding’s inequality

Recall that we assume in Section
4.5.1
that
Y(a_{1:t})
has the form
Y(a_{1:t})=f(X_{0:t}(a_{1:t}))
, and that moreover

y_{\mathrm{lo}}\leq f(x_{0:t})\leq y_{\mathrm{up}}\qquad\text{for all $x_{0:t}\in B_{0:t}$.}

(8.21)

This means
\widehat{Y}^{(i)}
is almost surely bounded in
[y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}]
, and so
\widehat{\mu}
gives rise to one-sided confidence intervals via an application of Hoeffding’s inequality.
The exact form of these confidence intervals is as follows:

Proposition 8.8.3
.

If (
8.21
) holds, then for each
\alpha\in(0,1)
, letting

\Delta\coloneqq(y_{\mathrm{up}}-y_{\mathrm{lo}})\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{2n}\,\log\frac{2}{\alpha}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{\Delta}\coloneqq(y_{\mathrm{up}}-y_{\mathrm{lo}})\,\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\widehat{n}}\,\log\frac{2}{\alpha}},

and similarly

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\coloneqq{\mu_{\mathrm{lo}}}-\Delta\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{R}^{\alpha}\coloneqq\widehat{\mu}+\widehat{\Delta},

it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Q_{\mathrm{lo}}\geq R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha})\geq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbb{P}(\widehat{Q}\leq\widehat{R}^{\alpha})\geq 1-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

Proof 8.8.4
.

We only prove the result for
R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}
; the other statement can be proved analogously.
Recall that
{\mu_{\mathrm{lo}}}
is the empirical mean of i.i.d. samples
Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i)}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
\mathbb{E}[Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i)}]=Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
(see (
8.20
)).
Moreover, by (
8.21
),
Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i)}
is almost surely bounded in
[y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}]
.
Hoeffding’s inequality then implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\mu_{\mathrm{lo}}}-Q_{\mathrm{lo}}>\Delta)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{2n\Delta^{2}}{(y_{\mathrm{up}}-y_{\mathrm{lo}})^{2}}\right).

In turn, some basic manipulations yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Q_{\mathrm{lo}}\geq R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha})

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}(Q_{\mathrm{lo}}<{\mu_{\mathrm{lo}}}-\Delta)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\exp\left(-\frac{2n\Delta^{2}}{(y_{\mathrm{up}}-y_{\mathrm{lo}})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\alpha}{2}.

8.8.4
Approximate confidence intervals via bootstrapping

While Hoeffding’s inequality yields the probability guarantees in (
8.18
) and (
8.19
) exactly, the confidence intervals obtained can be conservative.
Consequently, our testing procedure may have lower probability of falsifying certain twins that in fact do not satisfy the causal bounds.
To address this, we also consider an approximate approach based on bootstrapping that can produce tighter confidence intervals.
While other schemes are possible, bootstrapping provides a general-purpose approach that is straightforward to implement and works well in practice.

At a high level, our approach here is again to construct one-sided level
1-\alpha/2
confidence intervals via bootstrapping
(
Efron 1979
)
on
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
\widehat{Q}
.
Many bootstrapping procedures for obtaining confidence intervals have been proposed in the literature
(
Tibshirani and Efron 1993
;
Davison and Hinkley 1997
;
Hesterberg 2015
)
.
Our results reported below were obtained via the
reverse percentile
bootstrap (see
Hesterberg 2015
for an overview).
(We also tried the
percentile
bootstrap method, which obtained nearly indistinguishable results.)
In particular, this method takes

R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}\coloneqq 2{\mu_{\mathrm{lo}}}-\Delta\qquad\widehat{R}^{\alpha}\coloneqq 2\widehat{\mu}-\widehat{\Delta},

where
\Delta
and
\widehat{\Delta}
correspond to the approximate
1-\alpha/2
and
\alpha/2
quantiles of the distributions of

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i^{\ast})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{1}{\widehat{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{\widehat{n}}\widehat{Y}^{(i^{\ast})},

where each
Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i^{\ast})}
and
Y^{(i^{\ast})}
is obtained by sampling uniformly with replacement from among the values of
Y_{\mathrm{lo}}^{(i)}
and
Y^{(i)}
.
In our case study, as is typically done in practice, we approximated
\Delta
and
\widehat{\Delta}
via Monte Carlo sampling.
It can be shown that the confidence intervals produced in this way obtain a coverage level that approaches the desired level of
1-\alpha/2
as
n
and
\widehat{n}
grow to infinity under mild assumptions
(
Hall 1988
)
.

8.9
Experimental Details

8.9.1
MIMIC preprocessing

For data extraction and preprocessing, we re-used the same procedure as
Komorowski et al. 2018
with minor modifications.
For completeness, we describe the pre-processing steps applied in
Komorowski et al. 2018
and subsequently outline our modifications to these.

Following
Komorowski et al. 2018
, we extracted adult patients fulfilling the sepsis-3 criteria
(
Singer et al. 2016
)
.
Sepsis was defined as a suspected infection (as indicated by prescription of antibiotics and sampling of bodily fluids for microbiological culture) combined with evidence of organ dysfunction, defined by a SOFA score
\geq 2

(
Singer et al. 2016
;
Seymour et al. 2016
)
.

Following
Komorowski et al. 2018
, we excluded patients for whom any of the following was true: their age was less than
18
years old at the time of ICU admission; their mortality not documented; their IV fluid/vasopressors intake was not documented; their treatment was withdrawn.

We made the following modifications to the preprocessing code of
Komorowski et al. 2018
for our experiment.
First, instead of extracting physiological quantities (e.g. heart rate) every 4 hours, we extracted these every hour.
Additionally, we excluded patients with any missing hourly vitals during the first 4 hours of their ICU stay.

We then extracted a total of 19 quantities of interest listed in Table
8.1
.
Of these, 17 were physiological quantities associated with the patient, including static demographic quantities (e.g. age), patient vital signs (e.g. heart rate), and patient lab values (e.g. potassium blood concentration).
All of these were continuous values, apart from sex.
These were chosen as the subset of physiological quantities extracted from MIMIC by
Komorowski et al. 2018
that are also modelled by Pulse, and were used to define our observation spaces
\mathcal{X}_{t}
as described next.
The remaining 2 quantities (intravenous fluids and vasopressor doses) were chosen since they correspond to treatments that the patient received, and were used to define our action spaces
\mathcal{A}_{t}
as described below.

8.9.2
Sample splitting

Before proceeding further, we randomly selected 5% of the extracted our trajectories (583 trajectories, denoted as
\mathcal{D}_{0}
) to use for preliminary tasks such as choosing the parameters of our hypotheses.
We reserved the remaining 95% (11,094 trajectories, denoted as
\mathcal{D}
) for the actual testing.
By a standard sample splitting argument
(
Cox 1975
)
, the statistical guarantees of our testing procedure established above continue to apply even when our hypotheses are defined in this data-dependent way.

8.9.3
Observation spaces

Our
\mathcal{X}_{0}
consisted of the following features: age, sex, weight, heart rate, systolic blood pressure, diastolic blood pressure and respiration rate.
We chose
\mathcal{X}_{0}
in this way because, out of the 17 physiological quantities we extracted from MIMIC, these were the quantities that can be initialised to user-provided values before starting a simulation in the version of Pulse we considered (4.x).
(In contrast, Pulse initialises the other 10 features to default values.)
For the remaining observation spaces, we used the full collection of the 17 physiological quantities we extracted to define
\mathcal{X}_{1}=\cdots=\mathcal{X}_{4}
.
We encoded all features in
\mathcal{X}_{t}
numerically, i.e.
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\mathbb{R}^{7}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{t}=\mathbb{R}^{17}
for
t\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.

8.9.4
Action spaces

Following
Komorowski et al. 2018
, we constructed our action space using 2 features obtained from MIMIC, namely intravenous fluid (IV) and vasopressor doses.
To obtain discrete action spaces suitable for our framework, we used the same discretization procedure for these quantities as was used by
Komorowski et al. 2018
.
Specifically, we divided the hourly doses of intravenous fluids and vasopressors into 5 bins each, with the first bin corresponding to zero drug dosage, and the remaining 4 bins based on the quartiles of the non-zero drug dosages in our held-out observational dataset
\mathcal{D}_{0}
.
From this we obtained action spaces
\mathcal{A}_{1}=\cdots=\mathcal{A}_{4}
with
5\times 5=25
elements.
Table
8.2
shows the dosage bins constructed in this way, as well as the frequency of each bin’s occurrence in the observational data.

8.9.5
Hypothesis parameters

We used our held-out observational dataset
\mathcal{D}_{0}
to obtain a collection of hypothesis parameters
(t,f,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
.
Specifically, for each physiological quantity of interest (e.g. heart rate) in the list of ‘Vital Signs’ and ‘Lab Values’ given in Table
8.1
, we did the following.
First, for each
t\in\{0,\ldots,4\}
, we obtained 16 choices of
B_{t}
by discretizing the patient space
\mathcal{X}_{t}
into 16 subsets based on the values of certain features as follows: 2 bins corresponding to sex; 4 bins corresponding to the quartiles of the ages of patients in
\mathcal{D}_{0}
; 2 bins corresponding to whether or not the value of the chosen physiological quantity of interest at time
t
was above or below its median value in
\mathcal{D}_{0}
.

Next, for each
t\in\{1,\ldots,4\}
,
a_{1:t}\in\mathcal{A}_{1:t}
, and sequence
B_{0:t}
with each
B_{t^{\prime}}
as defined in the previous step, let
\mathcal{D}_{0}(t,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
denote the subset of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
corresponding to
(t,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
, i.e.

\mathcal{D}_{0}(t,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})\coloneqq\{X_{0:t}(A_{1:t})\mid\text{$(X_{0:T}(A_{1:T}),A_{1:T})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}$ with $A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}$ and $X_{0:t}(A_{1:T})\in B_{0:t}$}\}.

We then selected the set of all triples
(t,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
such that
\mathcal{D}_{0}(t,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
contained at least one trajectory.
This meant the number of combinations of hypotheses parameters that we considered was limited to a tractable quantity, which had benefits both computationally, and also by ensuring that we did not sacrifice too much power when adjusting for multiple testing.

Finally, for each selected triple
(t,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
, we chose a corresponding
f
as follows.
First, we let
i\in\{1,\ldots,d_{t}\}
denote the index of the physiological quantity of interest in
\mathcal{X}_{t}=\mathbb{R}^{d_{t}}
.
We then set
y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}
to be the .2 and the .8 quantiles of the values in

\{(X_{t}(A_{1:t}))_{i}\mid X_{0:t}(A_{1:t})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}(t,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})\}

We then obtained
f:\mathcal{X}_{0:t}\to\mathbb{R}
as the function that extracts the physiological quantity of interest from
\mathcal{X}_{t}
and clips its value to between
y_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
y_{\mathrm{up}}
, i.e.
f(x_{0:t})\coloneqq\min(\max(x_{t})_{i},y_{\mathrm{lo}}),y_{\mathrm{up}})
.

Overall, accounting for all physiological quantities of interest, we obtained 721 distinct choices of
(t,f,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
in this way.
Figure
8.2
shows the amount of non-held out observational and twin data that we subsequently used for testing each hypothesis, i.e. the values of
n
and
\widehat{n}
as defined in Section
8.8.2
above.
(We describe how we generated our dataset of twin trajectories in Section
8.9.6
.)

8.9.6
Generating twin trajectories using the Pulse Physiology Engine

The Pulse Physiology Engine is an open source comprehensive human physiology simulator that has been used in medical education, research, and training. The core engine of Pulse is C++ based with APIs available in different languages, including python. Detailed documentation is available at:
pulse.kitware.com
.
Pulse allows users to initialize patient trajectories with given age, sex, weight, heart rate, systolic blood pressure, diastolic blood pressure and respiration rate and medical conditions such as sepsis, COPD, ARDS, etc. Once initialised, users have the ability to advance patient trajectories by a given time step (one hour in our case), and administer actions (e.g. administer a given dose of IV fluids or vasopressors).

In Algorithm
3
we describe how we generated the twin data to test the chosen hypotheses. Note that we sampled
X_{0}
without replacement as it ensures that each
X_{0}
is chosen at most once and consequently twin trajectories in
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t})
are i.i.d.
Additionally, Algorithm
3
can be easily parallelised to improve efficiency.
Figure
8.2
shows histograms of the number of twin trajectories
\widehat{n}
(as defined in Section
8.8.2
above) obtained in this way across all hypotheses.

Inputs:
Action sequence
a_{1:t}
; Observational dataset
\mathcal{D}
.

Output:
Twin data
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t})
of size
m
.

for

i=1,\dots,m

do

Sample
X_{0}
without replacement from
\mathcal{D}
;

\widehat{X}_{0}\leftarrow X_{0}
i.e., initialize the Pulse trajectory with the information of
X_{0}
;

for

t^{\prime}=1,\dots,t

do

Administer the median doses of IV fluids and vasopressors in action bin
a_{t^{\prime}}
;

if

t^{\prime}\equiv 0

(mod 3)

then

Virtual patient in Pulse consumes nutrients and water, and urinates;

end if

Advance the twin trajectory by one hour;

end for

Add the trajectory
\widehat{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})
to
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t})
;

end for

Return

\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t})

Algorithm 3

Generating Twin data
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(a_{1:t})
.

and

(as defined in Section
8.8.2
) across all hypothesis parameters corresponding to each physiological quantity of interest.

8.9.7
Bootstrapping details

In addition to Hoeffding’s inequality, we also used reverse percentile bootstrap method (see e.g.
Hesterberg 2015
) to obtain our confidence intervals on
Q_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
Q_{\mathrm{up}}
as described in Section
8.8.2
.
We used 100 bootstrap samples for each confidence interval.
To avoid bootstrapping on small numbers of data points, we did not reject any hypothesis where either the number of observational trajectories
n
or twin trajectories
\widehat{n}
was less than 100, and returned a
p
-value of 1 in each such case.

Table
8.4
shows the number of rejected hypotheses for each physiological quantity using this approach.
We observed a similar trend as in our results obtained using Hoeffding’s inequality (Table
8.3
).
For example, we obtained high number of rejections for Sodium, Chloride and Potassium blood concentrations but few rejections for Arterial Pressure and Heart Rate.
Overall, bootstrapping increased the number of rejected hypotheses by a factor of roughly 3.3 compared with Hoeffding’s inequality (281 vs. 85 rejections in total).
Like we described for Hoeffding’s inequality in the main text, we also ran this analysis with each hypothesis obtained using the unconditional bounds of
Manski 1990
, and again obtained substantially fewer rejections compared with our approach based on Theorem
4.4.1
.

:

A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}
and

, and from the output of the twin conditional on
\widehat{X}_{0:t}(a_{1:t})\in B_{0:t}
.

(B_{0:t},a_{1:t})
.
Below each figure are shown 95% Hoeffding confidence intervals for
\widehat{Q}
and
Q_{\mathrm{up}}
.
Unlike Figure
4.3
from the main text, the horizontal axes of the histograms are not truncated, and the first row is in particular an untruncated version of Figure
4.3
from the main text.
Note however that the scales of the horizontal axes of the confidence intervals differ from those of the histograms, since it is visually more difficult to determine whether or not the confidence intervals overlap when fully zoomed out.

8.9.8
Tightness of bounds and number of data points per hypothesis

In this section, we show empirically how both the tightness of the bounds
[Q_{\mathrm{lo}},Q_{\mathrm{up}}]
and the number of data points per hypothesis relate to the number of falsifications obtained in our case study.
Recall that the tightness of
[Q_{\mathrm{lo}},Q_{\mathrm{up}}]
is determined by the value of
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})
, since we have

\frac{Q_{\mathrm{up}}-Q_{\mathrm{lo}}}{y_{\mathrm{up}}-y_{\mathrm{lo}}}=1-\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N}).

(8.22)

Here the left-hand side is a number in
[0,1]
that quantifies the tightness of the bounds
[Q_{\mathrm{lo}},Q_{\mathrm{up}}]
relative to the trivial worst-case bounds
[y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}]
, with smaller values meaning tighter bounds. The equation above shows that the higher the value of
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})
, the tighter the bounds are.

Figure
8.5
shows the bounds are often informative in practice, with
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})
being reasonably large (and hence the bounds tight, by (
8.22
) above) for a significant number of hypotheses we consider.
However, rejections still occur even when the bounds are reasonably loose (e.g.
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})\approx 0.3
), which shows our method can still yield useful information even in this case.
We moreover observe rejections across a range of different numbers of observational data points used to test each hypothesis, which shows that our method is not strongly dependent on the size of the dataset obtained.

8.9.9
Sensitivity to
y_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
y_{\mathrm{up}}

We investigated the sensitivity of our methodology with respect to our choices of the values
y_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
y_{\mathrm{up}}
.
Specifically, we repeated our procedure with the intervals
[y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}]
replaced with
[y_{\mathrm{lo}}\,(1-\Delta/2),y_{\mathrm{up}}\,(1+\Delta/2)]
for a range of different values of
\Delta\in\mathbb{R}
.
Figure
8.6
plots the number of rejections for different values of
\Delta
.
We observe that for significantly larger
[y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}]
intervals, we do obtain fewer rejections, although this is to be expected since the widths of our both the bounds
[Q_{\mathrm{lo}},Q_{\mathrm{up}}]
and our confidence intervals
R_{\mathrm{lo}}^{\alpha}
and
R_{\mathrm{up}}^{\alpha}
obtained using Hoeffding’s inequality (see Proposition
8.8.3
) grow increasingly large as the width of
[y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}]
grows.
However, we observe that the number of rejections per outcome is stable for a moderate range of widths of
[y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}]
, which indicates that our method is reasonably robust to the choice of
y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}
parameters.

Figure 8.5
:
Sample mean estimate of
\mathbb{P}(A_{1:t}=a_{1:t}\mid X_{0:N}(A_{1:N})\in B_{0:N})
for each pair of hypotheses
(\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lo}},\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{up}})
corresponding to the same set of parameters
(t,f,a_{1:t},B_{0:t})
that we tested, along with the corresponding number of observational data points used to test each hypothesis.
Red points indicate that either
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lo}}
or
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{up}}
were rejected, while blue points indicate that both
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lo}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{up}}
were not rejected.

:
Rejections obtained as the width of the
[y_{\mathrm{lo}},y_{\mathrm{up}}]
interval changes. Here, the interval is increased (or decreased) symmetrically on each side.
